SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
 Do you know the story of Aladdin ? In 
short, the story goes like this – the boy named 
Aladdin had a magic lamp. When rubbed with 
the hand, a genie comes out of the lamp. The 
genie helps Aladdin by doing all his works.
  Let us know about another story. A boy, in 
his dream, received a medicine from the God. 
A strange medicine, that can cure all diseases!
Do you think these to be true ? Surely, not. 
Are these science ? Well, these cannot be 
science at all. Then what is science?
1.1 What is Science?
 You definitely study book and gather 
knowledge from the books. Science is 
Fig 1.1: Aladdin’s 
genie - does it really 
exist ?
Fig 1.2: A sapling of a plant in 
a tub
1 Activity 1.1:
Have you noticed any incident which is not science? Mention 
some of these.
The Wonderful
World of Science1
basically knowledge. In order to gain this knowledge, you have to 
observe things minutely. We say that you are to make observations. 
Then you are to do experiments. Therefore,  science is knowledge 
perceived through observation 
and experiment.
 Just take an example of  a 
sapling. You can observe whether 
it grows in the sunshine or in 
the shade. Or, you can observe 
whether it needs water or grows 
in dry condition. Further, you can 
also observe how tall the sapling 
grows after every five days. Then 
you can plant a sapling in a tub 
and examine. Thus, through 
observation and experiment you 
will acquire knowledge regarding 
the growth of the sapling.

1.2 Scientific Attitude
 You have to prepare yourself for acquiring knowledge. How to 
do so? Firstly, you must learn how to doubt. Doubt means systematic 
or logical doubt. Let us consider one example. Suppose, all your family 
members are coming back home in the evening from somewhere. You 
have switched on the light but the light does not turn on. What will you 
do ? Perhaps you will switch the light on and off a few more times. If 
still the light does not turn on then you may try with the nearby switch 
of the fan. Suppose the fan also does not turn on. If that happens,  you 
may think that the problem is there with the electricity supply. Then 
you will look at the nearby houses. If the nearby houses are also in 
darkness, then you may be sure that the electricity is out. You see, how 
you are having doubts systematically. Again, whenever you start to 
doubt systematically, there will be a question. In this case, the question 
is ‘why the light does not turn on’?
 After systematic doubt, you are to  look for proof . There is no 
light in the neighbourhood. This is the proof that there is no electricity.
 Last but not the least, one has to be open-minded i.e. whatever 
you say is the truth – it should not be like that. You should listen to 
others also. You may like somebody, but you should not accept his or 
her bad mannerisms. You must be neutral. No prejudice should prevail.
Thus, you  have a scientific attitude if you
Fig. 1.3: Experiment done by 
Galileo- see the two stones 
¿ can doubt systematically
¿ look for proof, and
¿ have an open mind.
 A person with scientific 
attitude can do a lot of good things. 
Have you heard of Galileo ? He was 
a great scientist. Let me tell you 
about one of his discoveries.
 Consider one big and one small 
stone. Suppose, you are allowing 
these two to fall from a height. Which 
one will reach the ground first? 
Earlier people thought that the 
bigger one would reach the ground 
first. Galileo doubted the idea. He 
wanted a proof. He took two such 
stones and climbed up the leaning

Do you know who 
invented aeroplane? Two 
brothers – Wilbur Wright 
and Orville Wright. They 
were very inquisitive 
from their childhood. 
They would often separate 
the parts of the bicycle, 
machine etc. available 
in their home. They tried 
to understand how the 
different  parts work. The 
two brothers loved to 
watch the birds fly. They 
often went outside to ‘see 
the wind’ when a storm 
came.
tower situated in the city of Pisa in Italy. Then he released the two stones 
simultaneously. What happened? The two stones reached the ground 
simultaneously. Thus, the truth was established. The earlier belief was 
proved to be wrong.
Fig 1.4: Flying bird and Aeroplane - look at their wings 
Fig 1.5: First Aeroplane -  Wright brothers 
are the first person to fly an aeroplane (1903)
1.3 You Can Be A Scientist Too
You know, scientists discover new things or new theories. Mobile 
phone, car, aeroplane, cooking gas, medicine etc. are some of the con -
tributions of science. You will find many of these in your homes. Do 
you also want to be a scientist? What should you do for that ?
The first step is of course observation. Do you see any similarity 
between bird and aeroplane as shown in Fig. 1.4, both fly in the sky ? 
Birds fly in sleeping position. They do not fly with head upwards and 
tail downwards. An aeroplane also flies like that.

Why do birds fly in sleeping position? In sleeping position, they 
experience less obstruction from wind. This is hypothesis. By hypothesis, 
we mean a guess regarding the cause of an incident. Finally, you are to do  
an experiment and verify whether your hypothesis is correct or wrong. 
In this way you can find out the truth and  such truth is knowledge.
Observation, hypothesis and experiment – these three together 
form the scientific method. For acquiring knowledge, scientific method 
is very essential.
It is not that only the scientists need scientific method. This is 
for all. This is even practiced by people of different walks of life. For 
example, if the tyre of your bicycle is flat, you take it to the person who 
can repair it. Firstly, he inspects and tries to find out why the tyre is flat. 
Then he finds out the hole by doing experiment using water. At last, he 
seals the hole so that no air leaks.
Similarly, whenever you have fever, your parents take you to the 
doctor. Fever occurs due to different diseases. The doctor observes you 
and diagnoses the disease from the symptoms. The doctor then  advices 
blood tests to detect the actual disease.

1.4 Wonderful Science
1 Activity 1.2: 
Have you noticed any incident where scientific method has been 
followed? Note it down.
Fig 1.8: Kon-Tiki expedition - 
Voyage to furore pacific ocean with 
old type yacht
 There are some islands, 
in the Pacific Ocean, very 
far from South America. The 
culture of the people of those 
islands are similar with that of 
the natives of South America. 
This is an observation. It was 
hypothesized that people from 
South America moved to those 
islands many years ago. But the 
Pacific Ocean is very rough. 
With the primitive boats it was 
next to impossible to travel such 
long a distance. Therefore some people considered the hypothesis to be 
wrong. Some scientists wanted to prove the validity of the hypothesis. 
They built a boat of primitive nature and started their journey. After 101 
days, they arrived at the island. Thus, the hypothesis was proved to be 
true. This is known as the Kon – Tiki expedition.
 You were a small baby 
at the time of birth. You were 
grown up with the loving care 
of your parents in their laps. It is 
difficult to take you on their laps 
now. You have grown up. In fact, 
all of us were born as babies and 
then slowly grew up. A sapling 
also grows into a big tree in due 
course of time. We take food 
that helps us to grow. Does the 
Fig. 1.9: Different stages of 
growth of plants
sapling take any food ? Science can provide you this answer.
We take different types of food. Besides, people of different 
places have different food habits. For example, our main 
food is rice. But the people of Punjab, roti is the staple food.
Nowadays many different types of food are available in the market. 
But what should we eat ? We should take that food which helps us to

 We cannot live without water. Water precipitates as rain from 
the sky. Have you ever wondered how water falls from the sky. 
Occassionally, hail also falls along with rain. Where does the hail come 
from? After washing the clothes your mother hangs the wet clothes 
outside to dry. Where does the water go from the clothes? While 
cooking, the vegetables in the pan are covered with a lid. After some 
time you see water drops accumulate on the lid. How do the water drops 
form? The top of the Himalayas and the Polar Region are covered with 
ice. What is the reason behind this? See, the amount of things we need 
to know.
 How far is your school from your home? Is it 1 kilometer away? 
Or 2 kilometers ? Or more or less than that ? Here, the distance of school 
from your home actually refers to the length of the road. Suppose, there 
are two roads leading to the school from your home. Let the distance 
to the school along the two roads be different. In that case, the lengths 
of the two roads will also be different. The distance from Guwahati to 
Delhi by train is nearly 1,900 kilometer. Suppose you are travelling by

speed. Then you will need 
nearly 5 months to arrive at 
the Moon.

like a blue marble in space. And we live on this blue marble. There are 
so many different types of living beings with us. Out of these some are 
plants while others are animals. How did life originate on this Earth? 
You have certainly heard about ‘aliens’. Is there any ‘alien’ in some 
planet? The scientists are pursuing studies in this regard also.
In addition to the living bein gs, there are many different types of 
matter in the earth. Just look around your home. You will find lots of 
things like rice, dal, sugar, salt, milk, oil, paper, pen, pencil, knife, dish 
– bowls (kahi-bati in Assamese) etc. Rice is a different matter, dal is 
again different, and so on. Each one is a different substance or matter. 
Why do they become different ? What type of matter constitutes each 
one of these ? Again, say sugar has been mixed with sand. How can you 
separate these two ? You will find solution to that in science.
Fig. 1.11: Blue marble and Aliens. The astronauts see the Earth 
as a blue marble
Fig. 1.12: Compass
Ships sail on the sea. Do you 
know how the sailors navigate the 
ship in the required direction ? They 
use an instrument called compass. 
The compass contains magnet which 
is the main constituent. A magnet 
always points north and south when 
suspended freely. Thus, it becomes 
possible to find out the direction. 
There are many things about magnet 
which you should know. For example,

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
 Our planet Earth is a wonderful place for organisms to live. You 
certainly know something about living organisms. You see various 
plants, birds, insects and other kinds of animals around you, don’t you? 
All of these are living organisms. The organisms perform life processes 
themselves to remain alive. Have you observed what happens when 
a seed germinates? After germination, doesn’t it become a seedling? 
The seedling grows into a tree, bears flowers and then produces fruits. 
Eventually, it dies at a specific time. What do you understand from it? 
Organisms have a specific life span and it differs from one organism to 
another.
2.1 Biodiversity and its meaning 
 While observing our surrounding, you can see various organisms. 
Have you ever gone for a walk by the river side or hilly areas? Have 
you noticed various trees, birds etc. over there? During your school 
vacations, when you visit the gardens or forests you will see different 
kinds of organisms. Some of them are trees and herbs, which we call 
plants. Forest have different kinds of plants. Various kinds of birds, 
butterflies and animals are also there. The colours of birds are different 
and their tweets are also different. Some birds are big and while others 
are small. Many insects live on the trees. Their structures and colours 
are also different.  
 The wings of butterflies are colourful, aren’t they? In our 
environment, we find many multicoloured, charming butterflies. Insects, 
butterflies, birds live on trees. Animals like snakes also live in the forest.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
2.2 Significance of biodiversity
We have a great relationship with the organisms found in our 
surroundings. Do you observe these relationships? Is there any 
relationship between birds and crops? What is the relation of a butterfly 
with flower? You know that birds consume crops as food, while others 
help crops by eating the insects that cause damage to them. Butterflies 
feed on the nectar of flowers and as a result they help in plant reproduction. 
Fruits serve as a primary food source for some animals. Think about a 
large tree! Birds build their nests on these trees to live. Insects on trees 
are eaten by birds. As a result, the number of these organisms remain 
balanced. Some birds eat the fruits of trees. The seeds of the fruit come 
out along with the faeces and fall elsewhere. From these seeds, new 
plants grow. In this way the number of trees increases. Birds thus help 
in the extension of forests in this way. Forests provide shelter to the 
animals. Plants in turn provide food to animals, provide oxygen for 
breathing. Animals release carbon dioxide gas during exhalation. This 
gas is used by plants for synthesis of food.
Fig. 2.1 : Organisms thriving in different environment

This way our environment remains balanced. Some tribal folk live 
in bordering places of forests. Have you visited such places?  They 
depend on the forest for their livelihood. Their tradition and belief have 
link with the forest. So, you should know that peoples’ culture and 
tradition is intimately linked with biodiversity. 
From where do you get the things to eat as food everyday? From 
where do you get the things for your everyday use? 
1 Activity 2.2: 
Make a list of things that you get from the forest and show them 
to your teacher. 
2.3 Diversity of animals and plants in our surroundings
 We discussed about visiting a garden or a forest, didn’t we? What 
did you learn by preparing the activity table in activity 2.1? You must 
have observed the characteristics of the plants in the forest. What do 
you find by observing the characteristics of these plants? Let us select 
some characteristic features to make things easier to understand.  
 ¿	 Trees are tall or short?
 ¿	 Leaves are big or small?
 ¿	 Flowers bloom in the trees or not ?
 ¿	 What are the colour of the flowers ?
 ¿	 Do the flowers have a fragrance?
 ¿	 Plants are erect or do they climb up other trees?
In the same way you can make a list by observing the similarities and 
differences of the animals you observed. What about the characteristics 
that are easily seen in animals. 
 ¿	 Is the size of the animal big or small?
 ¿	 How do they move?
 ¿	 Where are the animals found : in the branches of trees or in 
the soil or near by a hole?
 ¿	 Whether feathers, hairs or scales are present in the body?
After observing the above characteristics and having them noted 
in your note book, what have you understood? You know that many 
organisms can be seen while going to a forest or garden. Some of 
them are plants and others are animals. There are different types of 
plants. Some are tall and some are big. These plants are called trees.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
Birds like crows, common mynas, sparrows, cranes seen generally 
in our surroundings are also components of our environment. Crawling 
animals like squirrels, calotes, brown mabuyas are also essential for the 
environment. Jungles are the habitat of these animals. They maintain 
the balance of the environment.
So, we should not destroy the forest. Due to deforestation the 
environment gets affected. It reduces the food and space for animals 
leading to their extinction.
(a) Rhino (b) Tiger
(c) Buffalo (d) Swamp Deer
Fig. 2.2 :Different animals found in Kaziranga National Park
Some of them are bushes and are comparatively short. These are called 
shrubs. Some are very short. These are herbs. Some plants climb upon 
other trees. These are called climbers. Plants that spread and grow along 
the ground are creepers.
 The structure of the leaves vary among different trees. Not all 
trees bear flowers. Among animals, some crawl on the ground. While 
others run or walk with their limbs. Birds fly with their wings.
 Have you ever visited Kaziranga National Park? In the park, 
you can see different types of plants and animals. Animals like one- 
horned rhino, deer, wild buffalo, tiger, wild elephant and monkey can 
be spotted there. Birds like Indian pond heron, parrot, crane, moorhen, 
hornbill, hargila, bortokola (lesser adjutant stork) etc. make the park 
very attractive. Large snakes like python are also found in Kaziranga 
National Park. The park is teeming with diverse plants and animals. 
These plants and animals are the  biodiversity of the park.

2.4 Classification of animals and plants
 There are many students in your school. Their ages are not the 
same. Students sit in specific classroom according to their age. There 
may be two or three sections in  a class and the students study in every 
section. Such a method gives every student the opportunity to study. It is 
also easier to find out a specific student. It helps us to know the students 
better, doesn’t it? In the same way we should know the organisms in 
our surroundings. Therefore, organisms are classified into groups on 
the basis of their similarities or dissimilarities in their characteristics. 
Organisms with the same characters are included in one group. This 
process is called classification. It helps us to understand the organisms 
better.

1 Activity 2.3:
 Prepare some cards with your classmates. Each card will have 
a picture of a plant. Now identify the similar plants from the cards. 
Make groups of similar plants. You may have several groups by this 
arrangement.
 What have you learnt from this activity? You have learnt that, 
the characteristics or features of different plants are different, some are 
big trees, while others are bushes. The trunks of some trees are very 
large, while others are small and thin. Some trees have more branches. 
Trees like betel nut and coconut do not have branches.
To understand better, do another activity. 
1 Activity 2.4:
 Explore the surroundings of your school or home with your 
friends. Observe the trunks, branches, leaves and flowers of various  
types of plants properly. Note down the names and features of the 
plants that you will observe. Now make a list like the one given below.
Table 2.3 : Name of different plants and some of their characteristics

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
You collected some data while 
visiting a garden. You saw varieties of 
plants, didn’t you? Some plants are tall 
with thick and hard stem. They bear 
many branches and twigs. This type of 
plant is called tree. In some plants, the 
stem is hard but not thick. The leaves 
and branches of these plants are not 
very much above the ground. These are 
called shrubs. For example, china rose 
is a shrub, isn’t it? Some plants are short, 
and their stem is soft and thin. These are 
called herbs. Grass and paddy plants 
are herbs. Have you seen betel leaf plant 
climbing on a betel nut tree? Betel leaf, 
bottle gourd, white gourd etc. climb on 
some support. These are called climbers. 
Some plants spread by creeping along 
the ground. These are called creepers.
Fig. 2.3 : Different types of plants
(a) Tree
(b) Shrub (d) Climber
(c) Herb (e) Creeper

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
1 Activity 2.5:
While visiting a garden haven’t you collected some fallen leaves? 
Observe these leaves carefully. Do you see some lines on the lamina 
of the leaves? These are veins and veinlets of leaf. The arrangements 
of veins and veinlets in a leaf is known as venation. The big vein 
from the base to the apex of the leaf is called the midrib. Leaf veins 
originate from midrib. In some leaves, veins originating from the 
midrib are parallel. This is called parallel venation. Banana leaves 
exhibit this type of venation. Similarly, the veins in the leaves of the 
paddy plant are also arranged parallelly. When you observe a leaf of 
a peepal tree, you will see net like pattern of veins which is called 
reticulate venation.
Fig. 2.4 : Different types of venation 
(a) Diagram showing reticulate venation
(b) Diagram showing parallel venation

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
Can we classify plants on the basis of venation?
The venation of plants like paddy, wheat, maize is parallel. The 
roots of these plants are fibrous. On the other hand plants like rose, 
guava, peepal have reticulate venation in their leaves. These type of 
plants have taproots. 
We have discussed venation pattern and root structure. Let’s do 
another activity. 
(b) Fibrous root(a) Taproot
1 Activity 2.6:
¿	 Find a place with leaf greens and herbs.
¿	 Using a ‘khurpi’, carefully dig out a few saplings of leafy  
 greens and herbs along with their roots. Ensure that, the roots  
 do not get damaged.
¿	 Clean the roots  by washing them with water.
¿	 Observe the structure of the roots. 
While observing, you will see that some plants have one main root 
from which some secondary roots originate. This type of arrangement 
of roots is called taproot system. For example, the roots of china rose 
exhibit this type of roots. On the other hand plants like grass have a 
bunch of thin roots arising from the base of the stem. These type of 
roots are called fibrous roots. The plants with reticulate venation tend 
to have taproot. Whereas plants (like grass) with parallel venation 
have fibrous root. 
1 Activity 2.7:
¿	 Keep chickpea and maize seeds in two separate bowls of water 
overnight. 
¿	 On the next day, wrap the seeds in a dry cloth or a piece of dry 
newspaper. 
¿	 Leave the seeds for 6-7 hours.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
Based on similar characteristics, that we have already discussed, 
plants can be classified into some groups. Similarly, other characteristics 
can also be considered for the same purpose. You have learnt that the 
main basis of classification is the similarities among the plants. 
As in plants, animals can also be classified by observing some 
similar characteristics. This process is called classification. In case of 
classification of animals, characteristics such as body structure, size, 
body movements, laying of eggs or giving birth to new ones etc. are 
taken into consideration. By considering some common characteristics, 
animals can be grouped into broad categories. Again, some other 
common characteristics of the group could be considered to form small 
groups. Such classification helps to study biodiversity.  
¿	Observe the seeds carefully. Both the seeds have seed coat. 
Gently remove the seed coat.
¿	Observe closely the internal structure of the seeds.
Fig.  2.6 : Monocot and dicot seeds 
Cotyledon
You have observed that the seeds have germinated. There are two 
fleshy parts inside the chickpea seeds. Each part is called cotyledon. 
It stores food for the growth of the germinating sprout. Plants whose 
seeds have two cotyledons are called dicotyledons or dicots. Maize 
seeds contain only one cotyledon. Plants bearing such seeds are called 
monocotyledons or monocots. 
 There is a relationship between leaf venation, root type and seed 
structure in dicot and monocot plants. Dicot plants have reticulate 
venation and a taproot system while monocot plants have parallel 
venation and a fibrous root system.
(a) Monocot seed (b) Dicot seed with cotyledons

The Save Silent Valley Movement : A victory of conservation
 This is the inspiring 
story of a forest in Kerala’s 
Palakkad district, known 
for its rich biodiversity 
and pristine beauty. The 
movement aimed to preserve 
the untouched splendor of 
the Silent Valley, a moist 
What do you understand from the pictures above? Share your thoughts  
with your teacher.
1 Activity 2.8: 
Various types of animals
evergreen forest, through the extraordinary efforts of general citizens. 
For a decade, these individuals with strong determination  opposed 
the construction of a hydroelectric dam across the Kunthipuzha 
River. They utilized every available means and ways, including 
petitions, judicial appeals, newspaper articles, letters to editors, and 
widespread awareness campaigns. Their relentless efforts culminated 
in the successful protection of the Silent Valley, a landmark victory 
for conservation in India

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
Dr. Robin Banerjee (1908–2003) was a renowned Indian 
naturalist, documentary film maker and   environmentalist. He 
is commemorated  for his extensive contributions to wildlife 
conservation and education. During a visit to Kaziranga National 
Park sometime in the 1950s, Banerjee fell in love with the wild life 
of Assam and decided to settle down at Golaghat, near Kaziranga.
 His passion for nature led him to produce 32 acclaimed wildlife 
documentaries, including ‘Kaziranga, Wildlife of India’ and Madame 
Pompidou Award wining ‘White Wings in Slow Motion’. He also 
authored numerous books and illustrated children’s literature. A 
recipient of the Padma Shri in 1971, Dr. Banerjee was instrumental in 
raising awareness about the rich biodiversity of Assam, particularly 
Kaziranga National Park. His home  at Golaghat was converted  to 
“Uncle Robin’s Natural History Museum”, showcasing artifacts, 
photographs, and paintings from his extensive career. The museum 
serves as a center for nature education and tourism.  
2.5  Adaptation of animals and plants
Animals use different modes of movement. When you observe, you 
will notice that deers have long legs, fishes have spindle-shaped bodies, 
frogs and toads have long and folded limbs. Do you know why this is so ?
An animal’s body structure is suitable for the environment where 
it lives. The size and shape of the body transforms accordingly. All 
organisms get themselves adjusted to live in their environment. They 
adopt various mechanisms to do so. These mechanisms are called 
adaptation. Have you ever observed fishes in an aquarium? The body 
structure of a fish is suitable to move in water.

Have you ever heard the 
croak of frogs in summer? 
The toads hop near ponds 
in search of food. Their toes 
are connected by a web-like 
structure. These web-like feet 
help them to swim in water. 
While visiting a village, have 
you ever observed how the 
ducks swim in ponds? The 
webbed feet of duck help them 
to swim. Have you observed 
feathers of a duck ? An oily 
substance is secreted by their 
body to keep their feathers free 
from water. 
webbed foot
Fig. 2.7: A fish swims in 
water with the help of fins
Fishes have fins in their body. 
The fins help fishes to swim in water 
and to change the direction of their 
movement. Gills are present in both 
side of the head. These help to absorb 
dissolved oxygen in water. Their  
spindle-shaped body structure makes 
it easier to move through the water.
 The hilly places are cooler than the plains. In winter, snow falls 
in these places. The animals that live in hilly regions have their bodies 
covered with fur. The furs are long.They protect themselves from cold 
by using their long furs.
Fig. 2.9: To protect from cold, animals like Sheep and Yak’ s body 
is covered with fur 
Fig. 2.8 :  Webbed foot of duck

Have you ever heard of deserts? The desert environment is dry and  
sandy. During day time it is hot and the nights are very cold. Animals 
like snakes live in burrows during the day time to escape from the heat. 
At night they come out in search of food.
 Deserts have a very limited water supply. Hot deserts are 
characterized by scorching heat during the day and freezing temperatures 
at night. As a result, the animals and plants that thrive there are well-
adapted to survive both in intense heat and cold. 
 Camels in hot deserts have long legs with wide hooves. These 
features help camels walk on sandy terrain without sinking. In contrast, 
camels in cold deserts have shorter legs and are not as tall as their hot 
desert counterparts. The shorter legs make it easier for them to navigate 
the rugged and mountainous terrain of cold deserts.
 In deserts, food is scarce, and so camels store their food in 
their humps. Camels in hot deserts have a single hump, which helps 
them  to survive during  food crisis. In contrast, camels in cold deserts 
have two humps that shrink during late winter when food is limited. 
In addition, camels in cold deserts have long hairs from their head to 
neck, providing insulation against the harsh winters of Ladakh. Camels 
excrete minimal amount of urine, produce dry dung, and do not sweat. 
By conserving water, camels can endure long periods without drinking, 
making them well-suited to the desert environment.
Fig. 2.10: Camel of hot desert (left) and cold desert (right)

You have seen birds flying. How they adapt to fly ?  Their forelimbs  
are transformed into wings, which they use for flying. The bones of 
birds are hollow, thus reducing their body weight and making it easier 
for them to fly. However, Ostrich cannot fly. because their bones are not 
hollow. They are compact and that makes their body weight heavier.
Fig. 2.11 : Adaptation of birds for movement 
All of you have seen ponds, haven’t you? Some plants grow in 
ponds, such as- water lettuce, water hyacinth, water lily etc. These are 
aquatic plants. Have you ever wondered how these plants float in water. 
These plants are spongy and they contain air, which makes them light 
weight and helps them float in water. Their roots are short and grow in 
bunches.
(a) Flying Eagle (b) Ostrich

 You have learnt how 
does  camel, a desert animal 
adapt to its environment. Now 
let’s learn about a plant. The 
Cactus is a desert plant, with 
spiny structures. Their  spines 
are modified leaves. Leaves 
are modified to thorns in order 
to reduce water loss through 
evaporation. The stem is green. 
So it can produce  its own food. 
The stem of the cactus plant  is 
fleshy and serves as storage of 
water. Fig. 2.12 : Cactus
The trees growing in high mountains adapt themselves in their 
environment. In winter, snow falls in these areas. The trees are cone- 
shaped, allowing snow to easily slide off. Example - Deodar tree.
Fig. 2.13 : Deodar trees in the high mountains

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
 Rhododendrons are stunning flowering plants with bright 
flowers. To withstand the strong winds on the summits of the mountains, 
rhododendrons in the Nilgiri mountain range are shorter in height and 
have smaller leaves. However, rhododendrons are taller in the Himalayan 
region . Therefore, even plants like rhododendrons may display distinct 
characteristics in various locations to withstand the local conditions.
Rhino conservation of Assam
Assam’s rhino conservation has seen dramatic success despite 
near extinction levels in the past. By 1908, Kaziranga’s rhino 
population had dwindled to just 12. Even after that, Assam has 
achieved remarkable success in conserving the one-horned 
rhinoceros, primarily through the efforts taken up by the concerned 
authority at Kaziranga National Park, the natural home to most 
of the global population of this species. Through anti-poaching 
measures, community involvement, and habitat expansion, the rhino 
population in Assam has grown from 600 in the 1960s to over 4,000 
today. and from 1,500 in the 1980s to over 4,014 today. Initiatives 
like the burning of confiscated rhino horns, Assam’s zero-tolerance 
policy against poaching, making the state a global leader in wildlife 
conservation.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
You have already learned about organisms. The place where an 
organism naturally lives, is called habitat of the organism. Organisms 
acquire food from the habitats. They also reproduce in their habitats. 
Salim Ali  (1896–1987), 
widely known as the 
“Birdman of India,” 
travelled extensively 
across India to study the 
diverse bird species of the 
region. He meticulously 
documented their habitats 
and migration patterns, 
identifying areas with rich bird diversity. His efforts led to the 
establishment of conservation measures in significant locations such 
as the Ranganathittu Bird Sanctuary in Mandya, Karnataka and the 
Keoladeo National Park in Bharatpur, Rajasthan. Salim Ali authored 
a historic ten-volume series on the birds of the Indian subcontinent. 
In recognition of his contributions to ornithology and conservation, 
he was awarded the Padma Bhushan in 1958  and Padma Vibhushan 
in 1976.
Organisms living in land are called terrestrial organisms. Their  
habitat is terrestrial habitat. Forests, deserts, mountains are examples 
of terrestrial habitat.Organisms living in water are called aquatic 
organisms. Aquatic organisms live in fresh water or saline water 
habitats. The marine organisms live in saline water habitats. These 
habitats contribute to biodiversity. Some animals, such as frogs can live 
in water as well as on land . These are called amphibians.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
Sacred groves are undisturbed 
patches of forests that vary in 
size and  are found across India. 
They serve as home to diverse 
plants and animals, including 
many medicinal species. These 
biodiversity treasures are 
protected by local communities.
They strictly prohibit harm 
to animals, tree cutting, or 
disturbing these areas. Local 
communities. gather in sacred 
groves to perform various 
Traditionally Protected Forests: Sacred Groves
rituals and offer prayers, deeply rooted in their cultural and spiritual 
traditions. 
 In Assam and the other Northeastern states like Arunachal 
Pradesh, Meghalaya, sacred groves play a significant role in 
preserving nature and cultural heritage. Many such sacred forests are 
found in the Dima Hasao and Karbi Anglong districts. In many parts 
of Assam, it is also known as “Than”, while the Dimasa tribe in the 
North Cachar Hills refers them to as “Madaico”. These groves reflect 
the community’s deep-rooted respect for biodiversity and tradition.

What you have learnt?
¿ The variety of organisms found around us is a part of   
 biodiversity.
¿ The balance of the earth’s environement is maintained by  
 the biodiversity.
¿ Birds help in the expansion of forests.
¿ Organisms are classified based on certain characteristic   
 features.
¿ Forests, national parks etc. contribute to biodiversity.
¿ Organisms adapt to their environment by using certain   
 mechanisms.
¿ The habitat of an organism is the place where it lives.
¿ Habitats vary from environment to environment.
¿ Habitats enrich biodiversity.
¿ We should conserve forests to maintain biodiversity.

Exercise  
1. Darken the circle of correct answer
 i.  Butterflies help plants in 
   (a)    Adaptation   (b)  Preparation of food
   (c)  Reproduction   (d)  Breathing
 ii. Pomegranate plant is a 
   (a)    Tree   (b)   Shrub
   (c)  Herb    (d)  Climber
 iii.  Which of the following is not an aquatic plant ?
   (a)    Lotus    (b)   Jasmine
   (c)  Water lettuce  (d)  Water lily
 iv.  The wings of birds are  
   (a)    Modification of feather
   (b)   Modification of fore limb
   (c)  Modification of hind limb
   (d)  Both (a) and (b)
 v.  What is the function of long fur in the body of animals living 
in colder regions ?
  (a)   It protects the body from heat.
  (b)  It protects the body from rain
  (c)  It protects the body from cold
  (d)  It protects the body from injury

3.  Give short answers.
 (i)   What is called habitat ?
 (ii)  What is biodiversity ?
 (iii) How cacti are adapted to live in desert ?
(iv) Give three examples of plants having leaves with parallel 
venation.

4.  Write true or false.
 (i)   All insects are harmful for us.
 (ii)  Birds help in expansion of forest.
 (iii) There is no interrelationship among all organisms of the 
environment.  
 (iv)  The loss of biodiversity affects our environment.
 (v)   No organism can survive in extreme cold regions.
Extended Learning
¿ Observe a big tree in a nearby forest. Find out different types 
of organisms living on the tree. Make a list of these. Give 
your comment.
¿ Collect pictures of different ecosystems of the world. Paste 
these in a scrap book.
¿ Select at least fifteen different organisms that you know very 
well. Think about their interrelationship. Write a note about 
their interdependencies. Ask for help from your teacher, if 
required.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
Food for
Healthy Life3
 We all take food everyday. Food refers to an essential substance 
required for our survival. Food is needed by all living beings but the 
way of taking food is different. Green plants prepare their own food. 
Human beings and other animals depend on plants and animals for their 
food. 
 Why is food important for us ?  Food is one of the basic necessity 
of life. Food provides energy to the body for doing various activities. 
Food protects us from diseases and infections. Food helps in the growth 
and repair of our body. It plays a major role in proper functioning of our 
body. 
3.1 What do we eat?
 We eat different kinds of food to survive. Our food mainly 
includes rice, legumes, vegetables, fruits, dairy products etc. What 
are these food items and how do we consume them ? To know about 
different kinds of food items do the following activity. 
Table 3.1 : Food items consumed over a week
1 Activity 3.1:
 Make a list of the food items you consume over a week. After 
completing the table 3.1 compare your list with the ones that has 
been prepared by your friends.

 What did you observe from the data collected in Table 3.1? Do you 
eat the same kind of food everyday ? What differences and similarities 
do you observe in the food items consumed by you and your friends ? 
You may have noticed that you and your friend consumed a variety of 
food items.
 Do you think that diversity in food items exists in different states 
of india ? 
3.1.1 Food habits of different regions 
 India is a country that reflects “unity in diversity”. Here people 
live in different regions with different culture. Apart from culture and 
tradition, people of different regions have different food habits. Food 
habits are primarily influenced by the climate of the place, culture 
and availability of types of food etc. Thus, various crops are grown in 
different regions according to soil types and climatic conditions. 
Collect data of some food items along with the locally grown crops in 
various states of India.

3.1.2 : How have cooking practices changed over time ?
 Cooking is an art of preparing food by using heat to make it 
palatable and digestible. In India, food habits of different regions are 
different. The food choice as well as its preparation may differ from 
one another. The culinary practices and food habits have changed 
significantly over time. 
 Make groups of three or four from among yourselves. Now ask 
the adult whether there is any difference between food items of past and 
present. Have you ever helped in cooking at your home by any means ? 
Write the name of appliances used in the cooking process at your home 
in your notebook.  
    Earlier cooking was done by using earthen or bronze made 
pots, bronze utensils on small open fire furnance (chulha). Now-a-
days we use modern gas stove, microwave, air fryers for cooking. The 
earthen pots have been  replaced with pressure cookers and modern 
cookwares. Earlier grinding was done manually using sil-batta, stone 
grinder (chakki ) and husking pedal (dheki). Now-a-days we use the 
electrical grinder for grinding. These changes have evolved due to the 
advancement in technology, improvement in transportation and better 
communication.  
Fig 3.1: Changes in cooking appliances over time
3.2  What are the components of food ?
 Have you ever gone to school without taking food ? How do 
you feel if you cannot take a meal ? You feel weak, isn’t it ? To be 
healthy we should eat our food regularly because health is our wealth. 
The food we eat provides energy to our body. We eat different kinds 
of food and each dish of food comprises of one or more items such as 
rice, lentils, vegetables, oil, spices, fish, salt, meat, egg etc. These items 
contain some components which are very essential for our body. These

components are called nutrients. The nutrients contained in our food 
are discussed below :
 One of the major nutrients in our food is the carbohydrate. The 
primary sources of carbohydrate are cereals like rice, wheat, maize, 
millet; fruits like mango, banana, pineapple, grapes; vegetables like 
potato, sweet potato etc.
 Another kind of nutrient is fat. Fat can be obtained from plant 
sources and animal sources. Fats from plant sources include nuts such as 
walnut, groundnut, almonds; seeds like pumpkin seed, sunflower seed, 
flax seed, sesame seed etc. Olive, coconut and mustard are also source 
of fats. Some fats obtained from animal sources are ghee, cheese, fish 
etc.
Walnut
Groundnut
AlmondsCashew
Dry fruits
Fig. 3.3(a) : Some sources of fats

Carbohydrate and Fat provide energy for various activities in our body. 
Do you know ?
A lot of fats get accumulated 
beneath the skin of polar bears. 
This helps them to survive 
without food during hibernation. 
This fat serves as energy source 
during this period. 
Mustard oil Sunflower oil Almond oil
Fig. 3.3(b) : Some sources of fats
 Protein is another nutrient in our food. Proteins are obtained 
from plants as well as animals. Some sources of plant protein are pulses, 
beans, peas, nuts etc. Some sources of animal protein are milk, paneer, 
egg, fish and meat. Proteins are needed for the growth and repair of our 
body.
Fig. 3.4 (a) : Plant protein

Fig 3.4 (b) : Sources of animal protein 
Have you eaten mushroom?
Mushroom is a fungus. Edible mushrooms 
are the source of high quality protein. But it 
should be kept in mind that all mushrooms 
are not edible. Most of them are poisonous.
Do you know ?
Some insects are consumed by humans 
as food. These edible insects have 
great nutritional value. Insects are 
generally abundant with proteins, 
fats, vitamins and minerals. Few 
edible insect species in North Eastern 
Region of India are  paat silkworm, eri 
silkworm and muga silkworm; bamboo 
worms, crickets, honeybees, red ants, 
hornets, wasps etc. The term for eating 
insects is known as entomophagy. 
Some insect species are reared. Edible 
insects provide a healthy, nutritive and 
sustainable food option for consumers. 
Milk
Paneer
Egg
Meat
Fish
Cheese

blood.
 Different components present in food play a major role in 
our  daily life. In activities like walking, running, playing etc life 
requires a lot of energy. Moreover, various functions like breathing, 
pumping of heart, digestion of food etc that happens inside our 
body needs energy. This energy is provided by carbohydrates in 
our food. When more energy is produced than required, it is stored 
as fat. Fats serve as source of stored energy in our body. Therefore, 
carbohydrates and fats are termed as energy giving food. Besides this, 
fats help the body to absorb vitamin. It helps to keep our body warm.
 Proteins are needed for the growth and development of our body. 
Foods containing protein are often called body building foods. It is 
very essential for growing children to consume right amount of protein 
for their proper growth and development. Sports persons also require

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
large quantities of protein for building of their body muscles.   
 Vitamins and minerals are required in small quantities. These 
protect us from diseases. So these foods are known as protective food. 
 Besides these nutrients our body needs dietary fibers and water. 
Dietary fibers are also known as roughage. Though it does not provide 
any nutrient to our body but is an essential component of our food. This 
helps our body to eliminate undigested food. Whole grains and pulses, 
potatoes, fresh fruits and vegetables are the main sources of roughage.
 Water helps our body to absorb the essential nutrients from food. 
It also helps in elimination of some wastes from the body through urine 
and sweat. So it is very important to drink sufficient amount of  water 
regularly.  
Let us learn
Coluthur Gopalan (1918-2019) was an Indian Nutritionist 
who studied about food and nutrition. He is known as Father of 
Indian Nutrition. He analysed more than 500 Indian foods for 
their nutritional value. He initiated nutrition research in India. 
He was the man behind implementation of the Mid Day Meal 
programme in schools and took measures to  cure measles under 
various schemes. These schemes play a major role in improving 
the health and nutrition of millions of children in India.
3.3  How to test different components of food ?
 How do we know that the food we eat contains various nutrients 
? With some simple methods we can test whether cooked food or fresh 
food contains the major nutrients like carbohydrate (starch), protein 
and fat. Let us now perform the tests and record our observations. 
3.3.1 Test for starch 
 Carbohydrates are of many types. The main carbohydrates found 
in our food are starch and sugars. We can easily test if a food item 
contains starch with the help of the following activity.
1 Activity 3.2:
¿	 Take a small quantity of fresh food or prepared food such as  
 boiled rice, bread or potato.
¿	 Now put 2-3 drops of dilute iodine solution on it.
¿	 Observe if there is any change in the colour of the food item.  
 Does it turn blue-black ? Note the observation in table 3.4.

 Fig. 3.6 :Test for starch in food
1 Activity 3.3: 
¿ 		Take a small quantity of a food item for testing protein. 
¿ 		Make a paste or powder if the food item is solid. Put some of  
 it in a clean test tube. 
¿ 		Add around 10 drops of water and shake the test tube. Now  
 add two drops of copper sulphate solution to it. Again add 
  10 drops of caustic soda solution to the test tube. 
¿ 		Shake well and let the test tube stand for a few minutes. 
  A violet colour of the food item indicates the presence 
  of proteins. Keep record of your observation in Table 3.4 
  as mentioned in activity 3.2.

1 Activity 3.4:
¿ 		Take a small quantity of food item and wrap it in a piece of  
  paper and crush it carefully so that the paper does not tear.
¿ 		Now straighten the paper and observe.
¿ 		If you hold the paper against light, you will see the light 
  faintly through the oily patch.
¿ 		An oily patch on paper shows that the food item contains fat.

         Table 3.4 : Some nutrients present in some food items 
3.4 Balanced diet 
 The food we normally eat daily is our diet. It is necessary to 
include all kinds of nutrients in our diet in right amount for growth and 
maintenance of good health. 
 A diet which contains all the essential nutrients like carbohydrates, 
proteins, fats, vitamins and minerals along with roughage and water in 
right amount is called balanced diet.
 The nutritional requirements are not same for everyone. The types 
and amount of nutrients in food depend upon some factors like age, 
gender, physical activities, health status, occupation etc.
Deficiency of one or more nutrients can cause disease or disorder in our 
body. Diseases that occur due to lack of nutrient over a long period are

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
called deficiency diseases. 
  If a person does not get sufficient amount of  protein in his/her food 
for a long time, he/she is likely to have stunted growth, discolouration 
of hair, skin diseases etc. 
  If the diet is deficient in both carbohydrates and proteins for a 
long time, the growth may stop completely. Such persons become very 
lean, thin and weak leading to sometime inability of body movement.
 In ancient times, the sailors often suffered from swollen and 
bleeding gums during long voyages. In 1746, during a voyage, Scottish 
physician James Lind observed that sailors who consumed lemons and 
oranges recovered from these symptoms. The bleeding and swollen 
gums are the symptoms of disease called scurvy.
 From the above case it is noticed that consumption of lemons and 
oranges cures scurvy. Vitamin C is present in citrus fruits like lemon 
and oranges which help in curing scurvy. That means, deficiency of 
Vitamin C causes scurvy. Similarly deficiency of iodine in food causes 
goitre. Consumption of iodised salt also can prevent the disease Goitre. 
 Deficiency diseases/disorders caused due to deficiency of 
vitamins and minerals are given in table 3.5 below.
Table 3.5 : Deficiency diseases and disorders due to deficiency of 
vitamins and minerals in food.
Vitamin/
Minerals Deficiency diseases Symptoms
Vitamin A Loss of vision
Poor vision, night blindness, 
sometimes complete loss of 
vision
Vitamin B Beri-beri
Weak muscle, tingling and 
burning sensation of feet and 
hands, trouble in breathing.
Vitamin C Scurvy Bleeding gums, slow healing of any wound
Vitamin D Rickets Soft and bent bones
Calcium Bone and tooth decay Weak bones, tooth decay
Iodine Goitre Swollen neck, Mental disability in children
Iron Anaemia Weakness

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
 Do you like to eat potato chips ? These are good in taste but harmful 
to our health. These are fried food with high calories. The food rich 
in fat can be very harmful for us. Candy bars and sugary drinks also 
contain high sugar and unhealthy fat. These foods are low in vitamins, 
minerals and dietary fibers. These type of foods are called 'junk foods'. 
Consuming too much junk food makes a person obese and vitamin 
deficient. 
  To have a healthy body we should have a balanced diet and avoid 
junk food.

 Millets are one of the oldest 
food and possibly the first cereal 
grain known to human. Millets 
are diverse species of small 
seeded grass that are commonly 
cultivated. Millets are the major 
source of energy and nutrients, 
so millets help in overcoming 
malnutrition. Millets are tasty 
grains that have mildly sweet and 
nut like flavour. As the nutrient 
contents in millets are amazing, 
it is commonly known as 'nutri 
cereals'. Millet is rich in proteins, 
fibres, calcium, iron, potassium, 
magnesium etc. Hence they are 
called 'superfoods'. Millets are 
 Do you know ?
The United Nations General Assembly declared 2023 as 
International Millet Year. For popularisation of millet crop 
and food prepared from millet, the Government of Assam has 
already initiated Millet Mission for seven years (2022-2029).  
             Fig 3.8:  Millet
3.6 : Food miles : From farm to our plate 
 Have you ever visited an agricultural field? How does the food 
grain reach our plate from the field ? Various steps are involved from 
the time of sowing of the seeds to germination and till harvesting the 
yield. The harvested yield passes through some processes to reach the 
retail shop and finally to our plate. It also involves the efforts of many 
people.
 Let us learn about the various processes involved in cultivation 
of rice from the figure 3.9 given in the next page.
crops best suited for different climatic conditions. It requires less water, 
manure and pesticides.
  Some of the various types of millets are sorghum, finger millet, 
foxtail millet etc. In India millet is popularly known as Sri Anna. Find 
out the name of the types of millet cultivated in Assam.

Planting of 
Crops
Harvesting Thrashing
Winnowing
Granary for

Husking
TransportationRetail Shop
Rice on 
Plate
     Rice from farm to plate
  Fig 3.9 : Food from farm to plate
 The food mile refers to the entire distance that a food (rice or 
any other food/grain) travels from the farm to the plate. It can also 
be expressed as the distance travelled by the food from producer to 
consumer. 
 For any food grain, it is  important to reduce food mile. Reducing 
food mile helps to minimise the cost and pollution during its transport. 
Locally produced food also reduces the food mile. So, we get these 
foods at low cost, causing less environment pollution. It supports local 
farmers and keeps our food fresh and healthy. So try to consume the 
foods locally produced in your area. Write down the name of some 
foods which are produced in your locality. 
Let us learn
Food is wasted by people when left unconsumed by them on their 
plates. If we take as much food as we can consume, it will reduce 
wastage of food. Before wasting, we should always realise the 
efforts and time of the farmers and other community people 
involved to get the food from the farm to our plate.

What you have learnt ?
¿ Food provides energy, protects from diseases, supports growth 
and development, repairs and maintains our body.  
¿ The major types of nutrients in our food are 
carbohydrates,proteins, fats, vitamin and minerals, dietary 
fibres and water. 
¿ Carbohydrates and fats are called energy giving food. Proteins 
are called body building foods; vitamins and minerals are 
protective foods.
¿ A diet which provides all the essential nutrients in the right 
quantities along with adequate roughage and water is known 
as balanced diet.
¿ Deficiency of nutrients in diet for a long time leads to deficiency 
diseases or disorders. 
¿ Junk foods lead to obesity and deficiency diseases as they are 
high in sugar and unhealthy fat and low in vitamins, minerals 
and dietary fibres.

Exercise
1. Find the odd one out and give reasons :
(i) Sorgham, Finger millet, Foxtail millet
(ii) Kidney beans, Green gram, Soyabean and Rice 
2. Discuss the traditional versus modern culinary practices in India.
3. All delicious foods are not necessarily healthy while not all nutritious 
food taste good. Discuss with few examples. 
4. Three food items are given below 
 (i) Canned fruit juice
 (ii)  Fresh fruit juice
 (iii) Fresh fruit
 Which food item would you prefer to eat and why ?
5. Sugar is an example of carbohydrate. If sugar is tested with iodine 
solution it does not change its colour to blue-black. What can be the 
possible reason ? 
6. Why are millets considered as a healthy choice of food ? Can millet 
consumption alone suffice for the nutritional requirement of the 
body. Discuss.
¿ Millets provide nutritional requirements for normal 
functioning  of our body. Hence millets are known as 
nutricereals.
¿ It is always good to eat locally grown plant based food. 
¿ The distance travelled by a food item from the producer to 
consumer is known as food mile.

[c] Refrigerator [d]  Almirah
                     Fig. 4.1 : Some objects where magnet is used
 Open your pencil box, can you lift the lid easily ? Don’t you have 
to exert little force to open the door of the refrigerator in your house?  
Why does this happen? Actually, lids in some pencil box, doors of 
refrigerator, in toys, stickers, doors of house or cupboard magnets are 
attached. Iron or objects made of iron (such as iron needles, alpins, nails 
etc.) cling to magnets. Infact, magnet is such a substance that attracts 
iron like substances. Do you know about one more important property 
of magnet ? Suppose you hang a magnet with the help of a thread, you 
will see that its one end keeps pointing towards north while the other 
end  towards south. This makes determination of direction easier. Think 
of a ship sailing on a vast sea. Do you know how sailors determine the 
direction of the sailing ship ? They use a tool called compass. It consists 
of a needle shaped magnet fixed within it which can rotate freely. Now 
you can understand how a sailor determines direction while sailing the 
ship, don’t you ?

There is a story about the discovery of magnet. About 4000 years 
ago, there was a shepherd named Magnus in Greece. He always carried 
a wooden stick with him while tending sheep. The wooden stick had an 
iron cap at the rear end. Do you know what happened one day? While 
grazing, the tip of his stick got stuck to a large black rock. He could take 
out the stick only after pulling hard. Why did this happen ? It is sure that 
the stone was attracting the iron cap. In this way people came to know 
about the things that can attract iron. This stone that attracted iron was 
called magnetite or loadstone, after the name of the shepherd. This is 
how the English name magnet was derived.
Magnetite is a magnet which is found in nature. Magnets found 
in nature are called natural magnets or loadstones. On the other hand, 
a substance (such as iron) that is attracted by a magnet can be turned 
into a magnet. Such magnets are called artificial magnets. Artificial 
magnets are used in the lids of pencil boxes, toys, laboratories etc.
Fig. 4.2 : Picture of shepherd
                                Do you know ?
Around 600 BC the magnetic properties of loadstone were known 
in India. The well-known ancient physician and surgeon Sushruta 
applied it to draw out metal splinters from bodies of injured soldiers. 
The ancient Indian medical text Sushruta Samhita describes the use 
of magnetite to remove arrows embeded in a person’ s body.
 Some magnets possess magnetic properties for long time. Such 
magnets are called permanent magnet. Just keep a piece of iron or an 
iron article near to a strong magnet. No doubt, the magnet will attract

The list shows that all the substances are not attracted by magnet. Those 
which are attracted by magnet are called magnetic materials, such as - 
iron, nickel, cobalt etc. On the other hand, matters that are  not attracted 
by magnet are called non-magnetic substances. Examples - wood, 
bamboo, rubber, mirror, plastic etc.
  Fig. 4.4 : Attraction of iron particles by magnet of different shapes     
 The magnets used in the two activities are of different shapes. It 
is found that magnets attract iron even if their shapes are different. This 
means that the properties of the magnet remain the same even if the 
shape of magnet is different.
(a) Sand on a paper 
(b) Iron particles on a bar magnet 
(c) Iron particles on a horseshoe magnet
1 Activity 4.2:
 Take a little dry sand on a piece of paper. Now rub the sand 
with a bar magnet. What do you see?
You will see some black particles sticking to the magnet. These are 
nothing but iron particles. Ordinary sand is generally mixed with iron 
particles. Since the magnet attracts iron, the iron particles stick to the 
magnet.
1 Activity 4.3:
 Now try the same activity 4.2 with a horseshoe magnet. What 
do you see ?

       [ b] Iron filings on the two poles of a

Let’s play a game with magnets 
 Take the iron filings obtained from 
Activity 4.2 on a piece of white paper. 
Move the bar magnet under the paper. 
What do you see ? The iron filings are also 
moving with the magnet, isn’t it ?
    Fig. 4.5 : Game  with 
magnets
4.2 Properties of magnet
4.2.1 Poles of magnet
Fig. 4.6 : Determination of the poles of a magnet 
         [a] Iron filings on a white paper
          [c] Iron filings on the two poles of a horse-
shoe magnet
 You will see more iron filings  sticking to the ends of the bar magnet 
but in the middle part of the magnet the amount of iron filings will be 
very less. The horseshoe magnet will also have more iron filings at both 
ends. What do you understand by this?
 In case of magnet of any shape, the force of attraction at both ends 
must be strong, isn’t it? On the other hand, in the middle  part of the 
magnet the force of attraction  will be the lowest. The two ends of the 
magnet are called the poles of the magnet. One of these poles  is north  
pole and the other is the south pole. In short, the  north pole is marked 
as ‘N’ and the south pole is marked as ‘S’ (Be careful in using iron filings 
in the experiment as they are hazardous particles. You can also do the activity by 
using alpins instead of iron filings )
1 Activity 4.4:
 Take a bar magnet, some iron filings and a piece of white paper. 
Place the bar magnet on the white paper and sprinkle a little iron filings. 
Observe carefully. Repeat the process with a horseshoe magnet. What 
do you see ? Does the iron filings  stick to all parts of the magnet 
equally ? Do you notice any changes due to the different shapes of the 
magnets ?

111
SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
You have come to know that magnet have two poles. There may be 
attraction or repulsion between the two poles. Opposite poles of two 
magnets attract each other while like poles repeal each other. Let’s do 
an activity to know more about it.  
       Fig. 4.7 : Attraction and Repulsion of magnet             
       [a] Two bar magnets
(b) N-pole brought closer to N-pole [c] S-pole brought closer to N-pole
 What do you learn from the activity? Firstly, the properties of 
the two poles of a magnet are not the same. Secondly, there is repulsion 
between the same poles (north-north or south-south) of the two magnet.
But the opposite poles attract each other. You can play a game using this 
behaviour. Keep a bar magnet vertically in a small-mouthed bottle. Try 
to put another bar magnet in the mouth of the bottle. Bring the opposite 
poles of the two magnets closer to each other. The magnet inside the 
bottle will come up and stick to the magnet above. On the other hand if 
you bring the same pole, the upper magnet will float in air.
Fig. 4.8 : Attraction and Repulsion of magnet
1 Activity 4.5:
 Take a bar magnet on a smooth table. Hold the magnet with 
your left hand. Slide the north pole of another bar magnet near to the 
north pole of the first magnet  slowly. Do you feel something when the 
second magnet approaches the first one? Release the second magnet. 
What do you see? Has the second magnet moved away from the first 
magnet? Now bring the south pole of the second magnet near to the 
north pole of the first magnet. The two magnets cling together, isn’t it?

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
 Thus we can conclude that a 
magnet has two poles. If you divide 
a bar  magnet in the middle you will 
get two pieces of magnets. Each 
of these pieces will have a north 
pole and a south pole. If you divide 
further the pieces will become 
smaller. However, north and south 
pole will always be there in each 
piece. It is not possible to get a 
north pole or a south pole alone. 
This means that we can never get 
magnet with a single pole. Fig. 4.9 : Two poles of a magnet
[a] Bar magnet
[b] Bar magnet broken into two pieces
[c]  Bar magnet broken into four pieces
1 Activity 4.6:
 Look at Fig. 4.10. 
Tie up a bar magnet with 
a thread in the middle and 
hang it as shown in the 
figure. The magnet must 
remain in a horizontal 
position. There should 
not be any magnet or 
magnetic substance near 
the suspended magnet. 
Place a piece of white 
paper under the magnet. 
The magnet will come to 
a static position after the 
initial movement. Draw 
Fig 4.10 : Determination of 
direction by using magnet
4.2.2 Finding direction 
two points on the paper indicating the two ends of the magnet. Now 
join the two points with a line. The line will determine the direction 
of the steady magnet. Now hold one end of the magnet and change 
its direction. After a while the magnet will come to a steady position 
once again. Indicate the two ends of the magnet by drawing two 
points on the paper as before. Repeat the activity few times more. 
What do you find ? The direction of the magnet remains the same, 
isn’t it ?

[PAGE 124]
115
SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
So, we can conclude that a freely 
suspended magnet always aligns in 
particular direction. This is the north-
south direction. The end pointing 
towards north is the north pole of the 
magnet and the south pointing end 
is the south pole. Why does a freely 
suspended magnet always aligns north-
south direction ? This is because our 
Earth is like a huge bar magnet. This is 
called geomagnet.
Fig 4.11 : Geomagnet
1 Activity 4.7:
Collect a sewing needle, a bar magnet, a pencil and a thread. 
At first, place the needle on a wooden table. Place any one pole of the 
magnet on one end (say, pointed end) of the needle. Then rub the magnet 
over the needle to the other end. Lift the magnet and rub the needle 
from the same end to the other end as before. Rub the needle 30-40
times from the same end. Don’t rub in the opposite direction. Now 
Fig 4.12 : Picture of a 
compass
The south pole of the geomagnet indicates north while its 
north pole indicates south. So now you understand why a freely 
suspended magnet remains in the north-south direction, don’t you?
 Try again the above activity by using an iron rod, wooden or 
plastic scale instead of a magnet. Do they indicate north and south 
direction when they are at rest ? Is such a bar or scale a magnet?
 This property of magnet is used to determine direction on 
the earth’s surface. In ancient times, travellers used natural magnet 
for the purpose. They determined direction by suspending the 
natural magnet with a thread. This property 
of magnet is used to develop a compass.
 A compass is a small box with a glass lid. It 
has a small needle-shaped magnet in the middle. 
This needle can rotate freely. You know that, at 
steady state, the two ends of the magnet always 
point in the north-south direction. The north pole 
of the magnet is marked red. Thus from the needle 
of the compass we find north and south directions. 
This facilitates the determination of the other 
directions (east and west). This is the reason why 
sailors use a compass to ascertain directions at sea.  
4.2.3. Preparation of magnet

 It will be seen that the needle keeps 
pointing in the north-south direction when it 
is not moving. Even after knocking, it comes 
back to the same position once it stops. It 
means that the needle is acting like a compass.
 Apart from sailors, soldiers also use 
compass for various purposes. Nowadays 
with the help of new technology, the compass 
is widely used for many other purposes. For 
example, the digital compass is used in smart  
phones, GPS etc. use. 
       Fig. 4.14  : 
Determination of 
direction with the help 
of a needle magnet
Fig. 4.13 : Method of preparation
of magnet.
bring a pin or iron filings near 
to the needle. What do you see? 
The pin or iron powder sticks 
to the needle, isn’t it? In other 
words, the needle attracts the 
pin or iron filings. So the needle 
has become a magnet.
  Tie a thread in the middle of the needle. Tie the other end 
of the thread to the middle part of a pencil. Cut a plastic water bottle in 
the middle. The lower part of the bottle will resemble a tumbler. Now 
place the pencil horizontally on the mouth of the tumbler so that the 
needle keeps hanging inside the tumbler. Now check in which direction 
the needle points. Gently knock at one end of the needle so that it 
moves. When it comes back to its stable position observe the direction. 
Let us learn 
 A childhood incidence of the world famous 
Physicist Albert Einstein goes like this. When he 
was five years old, one of his relative gifted him a 
compass. Child Einstein observed the compass in 
various ways. He wondered why the magnetic needle 
always keeps pointing north-south direction. What 
type of invisible force is there that keeps the needle in that position. 
He became quite curious and wanted to know the reason. He started 
asking his father and uncle about this. Such questions led him to learn 
about electricity and some other scientific discoveries. A school boy 
gave him some books on mathematics, physics and geometry to read. 
For the curious nature of the child Einstein, the world has been able 
to get such a great scientist.

4.2.4 Attraction and repulsion by magnets 
Fig. 4.15 : Activity on the property of attraction 
and repulsion of magnet 
(a) Two toy cars with 
magnet  [A & B]
[b]  Opposite poles (S pole of car A and N pole 
of car B) are brought closer 
[c] Same poles are brought closer  
 In the first part of the activity, the two cars are getting closer 
because the north pole of the car ‘B’ is brought closer to the south pole 
of the magnet in the car ‘A’ . On the other hand, when the north pole of 
the car ‘A’ is brought near to the north pole of the car ‘B’, the two cars 
try to move away from each other. 
B A
B
A
B
1 Activity 4.8: 
 Take two toy cars. Label one car as ‘A’ and the other car as ‘B’. 
Tie a bar magnet to each car. At first, keep the south pole of the 
magnet of car ‘A’ in front part of the north pole of the magnet of car 
‘B’ and bring them closer to each other.. Observe what happens.
 Then place the rear side of the car ‘A’  in front of the front side of  
car ‘B’ and observe. What happens ? What difference do you observe 
in the movement of the two cars?

 This means that, there is attraction between opposite poles of  the 
magnets and repulsion between the same poles of the magnet. 
 Keep a bar magnet suspended horizontally with the help of a 
thread. Bring another bar magnet near to this magnet. Do the same 
experiment for several times. Do you get the same result every time?
Fig. 4.16 : Deflection of compass 
needle by magnet
1 Activity 4.9:
 Take a compass and a bar magnet. Keep the compass on a wooden 
table. Now bring north pole of a bar magnet near to the north pole of 
the compass. Then bring the south pole of the magnet near to the 
north pole of the compass. Write what changes you have noticed in 
the position of the compass needle ? You can surely explain why that 
happens. 
4.2.5 Magnets can interact through non-magnetic materials also 
1 Activity 4.10 :
 Do the activity 4.9 by placing a card (paper) between the compass 
and the bar magnet. Do you notice any change ? You can also try 
the activity with a thin sheet of wood or plastic sheet in place of 
the  paper. You will notice that magnets attract or repel through non-
magnetic objects, such as pieces of paper, plastic or wood. This means 
that magnet can also act through non magnetic materials. Another 
example of this has already been given in Fig 4.5.
Fig. 4.17 : Effect of magnet 
through non magnetic 
materials.

Fig. 4.18 : Patterns made by magnets
 [a] Magnetic rope [b] Magnetic flower 
Let’s make a pattern with magnets  
 Let us see if we can make something by using magnets. We can 
make some patterns using paper and paper clip along with a magnet. 
Two types of patterns are shown in Fig. 4.18(a) and (b). We can join 
some paper clips on a horse shoe magnet, one after the other. In this way 
we can get two chain like pattern as shown in Fig 4.18 (a). Similarly, we 
can make patterns of magnetic flowers. This can be done as follows.  
 Draw a picture of a flower on a paper. Then place some paper clips 
on top and a magnet underneath the picture of the flower. We will get 
the pattern of a lower. Try your own patterns according to your choice 
specially during leisure time.
4.3 Use of magnet   
 We use magnets for various purposes. For example, magnet 
is used in various types of toys, doors of cupboards, pencil boxes, 
refrigerators, electric motors, laboratories etc. Magnets are attached 
to the doors of houses and cupboards. As a result, the door doesn’t open 
on its own; it opens only when you pull it. Moreover, magnets are also 
used in some medical devices such as MRI machines. They are also 
used in microphones, speakers etc.

[PAGE 134]
125
SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
Magnets lose their magnetic properties when heated, hammered or 
dropped from above. They should be kept away from computers, 
television, mobile, cassettes, compact disc (CD), transistors etc to 
preserve magnetism.
4.4 Preserving magnet  
  A magnet should not be kept open. If the poles of a magnet are left 
open for several days, the magnetism gradually decreases. In case of bar 
magnets, the opposite poles of two or more magnets should be placed 
in the same direction. The magnets should be separated by a piece of 
wood. Each end of the array of magnets should be joined by soft iron. 
In the case of horseshoe magnet, a piece of iron should be kept attached 
to each pole.  
Fig 4.19 : Some ways of preservation of magnetism.
   Let us learn The debit or credit cards that we use in banks have certain lines 
drawn on them. These are actually some magnetic lines. The 
lines contain lots of information.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
What you have learnt?
¿  Magnetic substances are attracted to magnets, but 
  non-magnetic substances are not. 
¿  Magnets that are found in nature are called natural magnets,  
 and the artificially prepared magnets are called artificial 
  magnet.
¿  A freely suspended bar magnet always remains facing in   
 north-south direction.
¿  Every magnet has two poles - the north pole and the south  
 pole.
¿  The magnets are attracted by opposite poles and repelled by  
 the same pole. 
¿  Iron can be converted into a magnet by rubbing it with a 
  magnet.
¿  Magnets lose their magnetic properties when heated, 
  hammered or dropped from above.

1. Fill in the blanks.
(a)  Materials that are attracted by magnet are called_______
 
(b)  Magnets made artificially  for various purposes are called 
________  magnet .                     
(c)  Rubber is not a ___________  substance.
(d)  If we break a bar magnet equally into two pieces, we will get                          
_________  magnets.
(e)  Similar poles of two magnets _________  each other.
(f)  Magnets always have ____________  poles .
2. Put tick mark () for correct statements.
(a) Magnets attract all kinds of objects. 
(b) There is attraction between the opposite ends of a magnet. 
(c) Cobalt is a magnetic substance.  
(d) The bar magnet always remains facing in north - 
      south direction.
(e) Magnets lose their magnetic properties when heated.
3. Match column A with column B
 Column A      Column B
 1. Compass    (a) The first magnet discovered
 2. Iron nails   (b) The magnetic property remains   
                    for a long time 
 3. Magnetite   (c)  Is attracted by a magnet
 4. Permanent magnet     (d)  At steady state, it remains in        
                                                north- south direction.

 Extended Learning
1. Take 3-4 magnets of different shapes and some paper clips. 
Keep the magnets under a paper. Sprinkle the clips on the 
top of the paper and test whether each part of the magnet 
attracts clips uniformly or not. 
2. Take a paper glass, fill it with water and add glue to it and 
then stire. Mix some iron powder in the mixture. Put a mag-
net on the mixture and observe what happens .
3. Try to learn about maglev train. You are given a wooden 
scale, a bar magnet and some paper clips. With the help of 
these items try to make a model of maglev train.
4. An alpin has fallen down in a bottom corner of your study 
table. You are not able to lift it up by your hand. By what 
way you can lift the alpin up ?
5. Try to learn whether a magnet can be used to lift heavy ob -
jects up ?
6. Try to learn how magnet can be used in the field of medical 
treatment.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
What are the things that you bring with you to the school? You bring 
books, notebooks, pen, pencil, eraser, etc. right? In your classroom also 
there are certain objects like chairs, tables, chalk, duster, desk-bench. 
You also have many things in your house. These include food items, 
clothes, utensils, cooking gas, chairs, tables, beds etc. There are also 
many things around the house and the school. 
Definitely the colour and shape of all the objects are not the same. 
Do you feel the same when you touch these objects? Are not some 
objects lighter than others?
The objects, we use, are made up of certain materials (such as 
paper, wood, cloth, glass, cotton etc.). For example, the notebook is 
made up of paper. The notebook is the object and paper is the material.
The substance from which an object is made is called a material.
5 Objects Around Us
1 Activity 5.1:
Make a list of some objects around you and write down what 
they are made up of.
Table 5.1 : Objects and which material they are made up of.
5.1 How to classify materials? 
Have you noticed how your mother keeps the objects in the 
kitchen? She keeps biscuits, mudi, sandah, rice grains, dal, sugar, tea 
leaves, garlic, mustard oil etc. in separate containers. She keeps all the 
containers containing spices together. Different containers containing 
different dals (e.g. lentils) are also kept together. As a result she easily 
gets what she needs.
Object Made up of which material?

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
 You have learnt 
from the activity that the 
same object can be made 
from different materials. 
Similarly, different objects 
can be made from the same 
material. 
 The method of 
grouping objects is called 
classification. Materials 
can also be grouped in a 
similar way. 
               Fig. 5.3 : Mother cooking curry
1 Activity 5.3:
Write the names of materials known to you that are used to make a 
tumbler.
 Your mother cooks curries in a kerahi (pan). The kerahi is made 
up of metal. Hollow bamboo segment is also used to cook rice and curry. 
But can you always cook rice in the same bamboo segment? Surely not; 
it will burn. Do you chop fruits and vegetables? The knife used for 
chopping is usually made up of iron. Can the knife be made from wood 
or bamboo?
 The metal pan does not burn even if it is used repeatedly for 
cooking.  This is the property of metal. On the other hand, the hollow 
bamboo segment burns. That is also property of bamboo. The iron knife 
is quite hard. While a bamboo knife is not so. These are the properties 
of iron and bamboo.
 You drink water from a tumbler. What materials are used to make 
tumblers?
Fig. 5.4 :  Materials used to make tumbler

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
 What will happen if tumblers are made up of paper or cloth? In 
order to make a tumbler, material that can hold water must be used. 
 How to choose the material needed to make an object? You choose 
depending on the use of the object and properties of the material, don't 
you? 
Fig. 5.5: Grouping of object
5.2 Different properties of materials 
 Let us learn about some of the properties of materials.
5.2.1  External Appearance
 Have you seen a fresh nail? Again, have you  noticed the sharp 
side of a knife? The nail and the knife are made of iron. Kettles and 
saucepans are generally used for heating water. These are usually made 
of aluminium. Hence, you have come to know that iron and aluminium 
look different; i.e. iron and aluminium differ in appearnce. Similarly, 
copper has different external appearance; it is deep brown in colour. 
Wood and bamboo also have different external appearances.
 Sometimes, more than one type of materials are used to make an 
object. For example, metal and plastic are used to make a pen. 
 Look at Fig. 5.5. Classify the objects shown in the figure in 
different ways. You can  consider properties, such as colour, shape etc. 
 Hence, we come to know that objects or materials can be classified 
according to their properties.

 Hence, we find that different materials have different external 
appearances. Again, new spoons, plates, bowls and tumblers, made of 
steel, shine. This gentle shine is called lustre. An object or material 
with this property is said to be lustrous. Do you have any other lustrous 
objects in your house? Metals are usually lustrous.
Fig. 5.6 : Objects made up of metal- observe the lustre of each 
item
1 Activity 5.4:
Take one piece each of old iron, aluminium or copper wire, cold 
and a candle. Take a piece of sandpaper also. Rub each one with 
sandpaper. Now write your observation in Table 5.2.
Table 5.2 : Change in brightness
Object Change in brightness
Candle
Iron
Aluminium
Copper wire
Coal
 Fresh nails (iron) and kettles (aluminium) are lustrous, but these 
generally lose their lustre and appear dull after some days. This is due 
to the formation of a layer on the metal. The layer is formed due to the 
presence of oxygen and water vapour in the air. When we rub copper, 
iron and aluminium with sand paper they become lustrous. Can you tell 
why this happens? Objects without lustre are said to be non-lustrous.
 Note that not all lustrous objects are metal. Some objects are 
coated with other materials, like plastic. As a result, they may become 
lustrous.
