5.3.2 Hard and Soft material
 Some materials are very hard,  for example: iron. Iron is difficult 
to compress when you press by using hand. But the eraser that you use, 
can be easily compressed by using your hand. So, the eraser is made of 
soft material. 
 You cannot test everything by presssing it with your hands. In 
such cases, we can examine with a metal key. Some items can easily be 
scratched with a metal key, while the others cannot. Materials that can 
be easily pressed or scratched with a key are considered to be soft. On 
the other hand, materials that cannot be pressed or is difficult to scratch 
easily, is a hard material. These properties, however, are relative. For 
example, wood is harder than rubber but softer than iron. 
Table 5.3: Hard and Soft object
Objects Hard/Soft. The material that makes it
Chair Wood
Mattress
Almirah
Jute bag
Sweater
5.2.3. Transparency : Can we see through materials 
 Shopkeepers usually keep chocolates, biscuits, sweets and other 
food items in plastic or glass containers. You can see these from outside. 
Would you be able to see these items if they were stored in an iron or a 
thick paper made container? When you play hide and seek, where do you 
hide? Certainly you would not hide behind the window made of glass.
1 Activity 5.5: 
 Press the items mentioned below by using your hands. Now 
sort them out as hard and soft objects. Write down the names of the 
materials they are made from.

 We can see through some objects, such as air, glass and 
polythene. The objects or materials through which we can see are called   
transparent object or material. On the other hand, objects or materials 
through which we cannot see are called opaque objects or material.
Fig 5.8:  Transparent, translucent and opaque objects
1 Activity 5.6:
 Take two sheets of thick white paper and a torchlight. Soak one 
sheet with kerosene. Turn on the torch light. Now look at the torch 
light bulb through the paper soaked with kerosene. Then try to see the 
bulb through the paper, not soaked in kerosene. 
a) Torchlight through white paper. b) Torchlight through oiled paper.
     Fig. 5.7: Opaque and Translucent objects
A
BC
 Look at the figure given below and group the objects A, B, C as 
opaque, transparent and translucent. 
(B) .......................... (C) ..........................(A) ..........................
You will not be able to see the bulb through the white paper. This paper 
is opaque. Do you see the bulb through the oiled paper? Is it clearly 
visible? You will see the bulb through the oiled paper, but not clearly. 
Objects or materials through which we cannot see objects clearly are 
called translucent objects or materials.

5.2.4  Water-soluble and insoluble materials  
 In summer you surely drink ' sarbat'. Do you know how is it 
prepared? Take water in a glass and add a teaspoon of sugar and a little 
salt. Add a little lemon juice and stir well with the spoon. What do you 
observe? The crystals of sugar and salt gradually become smaller and 
smaller and eventually disappear. However, the sweet taste of sugar 
comes into the sarbat. The taste of salt will also be there. Although 
sugar and salt are present in the water; but they cannot be seen. The 
water remains transparent. We say that sugar and salt get dissolved in 
water meaning sugar and salt are soluble in water.
1 Activity 5.7:
 Take salt, sugar, sand, chalk pencil powder, sawdust, flour, 
some glasses and a cup.
 Take one cup of water in a glass. Add half a teaspoon of salt 
and stir with a spoon. Does salt disappear? Take the same amount 
of water in another glass. Now, put half a teaspoon of sugar and stir. 
Repeat the same with each of the above mentioned substances. Now 
fill up the Table 5.4 given below.
Table 5.4: Mixing of other materials with water
Serial 
No.
The amount 
of water
Material Quantity of 
material
The material 
disappeared/did 
not disappear in 
water
1 One cup Salt Half a spoon
2 One cup Sugar Half a spoon
3 One cup Sand Half a spoon
4 One cup Sawdust Half a spoon
5 One cup Flour Half a spoon
6 One cup Chalk pencil
powder
Half a spoon
 What have you seen? Some materials disappear when mixed 
with water. These materials get dissolved in water. These materials are 
soluble in water. Materials which do not disappear when mixed with 
water are insoluble in water.
 Many kinds of materials get dissolved in water. Therefore, water 
plays an important role in the functioning of our body.

(a) Glycerine in water (b) Oil in water
 Glycerine is no longer visible separately in the tumbler. This 
means that glycerine is soluble in water. On the other hand, the mustard 
oil forms a layer on the water. So it is insoluble in water.
 Similarly, some gases are also soluble in water. For example, 
oxygen gas dissolves in water. Animals in water take this oxygen gas. 
Do liquids like oil, vinegar, honey, glycerine etc. dissolve in water?
Fig 5.9 : Water soluble and Water insoluble materials
1 Activity 5.8:
 Take two tumblers, two teaspoons, a little glycerine, a little 
mustard oil and water. Half-fill both the tumblers with water. Add a 
teaspoon of mustard oil to one tumbler and a teaspoon of glycerine 
to the other. Stir both mixture by using spoon and leave them for 
some time. What do you observe?

5.2.5: Materials floating and sinking in water
 Have you ever placed a paper boat on water? The boat floats, 
does it not? Rafts made of banana tree and bamboo also float on water. 
But stones, knives etc. sink in water. So, some objects float in water, 
while some others sink.
1 Activity 5.9:
 Write down the names of some objects around you. Examine 
and write whether each one of these floats or sinks in water.
Table 5.5 : Objects that float and sink in water 
(a) Floating paper boat  (b) Bamboo and banana rafts

Object Floats/sinks in water
5.3.6. Heavy or Light 
 Take a piece of an iron and a piece of bamboo of the same size. 
Lift them with your hands. Just by lifting with your hands you can say 
whether the piece of bamboo is lighter than the piece of iron.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
 Whether an object is heavy or light, it can be expressed by a 
property called mass. Heavier object has more mass, while lighter 
objects have less mass.
 Take two empty tumbler of the same size. Fill one with water. 
Now lift the both tumblers with your hands. What do you feel? The 
tumbler filled with water is heavier than the empty one, right? This 
means that water also has mass.
1 Activity 5.10:
 Take three glass tumblers exactly of the similar size, a little 
water, little sand, some pebbles and a balance. Mark the tumblers as 
A, B and C. 
 Half-fill tumbler A with sand. Similarly, half fill tumbler B 
with water and tumbler C with pebbles. Lift the three cups one by 
one by using your hands. Guess which one would be heavier and 
which one would be lighter . 
 Now weigh the tumblers using the balance and compare with 
your guess.

1 Activity 5.11:
 You will need two glass tumblers, a lemon and a jug of water. 
Fill one tumbler with water to the brim. Now put the lemon in the 
tumbler. What do you see? Some water overflows from the tumbler, 
right? Fill the other tumbler with water, again to the brim. Try to pour 
more water into the tumbler. What will happen? Water will surely 
overflow.
5.3.7. Volume
 Every object occupies some space. Just look at the objects in 
your house. The boxes, cupboards, cricket bats, balls etc. in your house  
occupies some space, does it not? The bag that you bring to school 
also occupies some space. Therefore, if you keep your bag on the 
bench, there will be limitation of space able to sit on it. Similarly, your 
classroom or house occupies  some space. The house certainly takes up 
more space than the classroom, right? The volume of an object is the 
space occupied by it. 
 Why did the water overflow when the lemon was dropped in the 
first tumbler? This is because the lemon occupies some of the spaces 
inside the glass. The space occupied by the lemon is its volume.
Do you know ?
Sometimes in common language weight is used for mass as it is 
determined by weighting. You will learn more about mass and weight 
and their relation in higher classes.
Copyright-SCERT, Assam

 Why did the water overflow from the second tumbler ? This is 
because the  tumbler is full of water and cannot hold any more water. 
All the space inside the  tumbler is occupied  by the water. The space 
inside the  tumbler is the volume of the water.
Look at the figure given below and tell where volume of water is more.
 Notice the drinking water bottles 
around you. Some of them are small 
and some others are large. Have you 
noticed what is written on the label of 
each of the bottles. You will see that 
some bottles are labeled as 500 ml, 
some 200 ml, some others as 1L etc. 
These lebels refer to the volume of the 
water in the bottle. Similarly, bottles 
of oil, milk in store also display their 
volume on the lebel.
5.3.8 What is matter? 
 Now you know that water, sand and pebbles have mass. Each of 
them occupies some space, meaning they have volume. In fact, every 
substance has mass and volume. Anything that has mass and volume is 
matter.
 Mass refers to the amount of matter. That is the mass of an object 
is the amount of matter in the object. It is generally expressed in gram 
(g) and kilogram (kg) units.
 Anything that occupies space and has mass is matter or anything 
which has mass and volume is matter.

1 Activity 5.12:
 Take a glass tumbler, a large bowl (charia), a piece of paper 
and  water. Dry the tumbler properly. Paste the piece of paper at 
inside bottom of the tumbler using gum. Fill the bowl with water. 
Turn the tumbler upside down and gently press it into the water. 
Now, take out the tumbler and observe the piece of paper. Surely the 
paper is not wet. 
 Why didn't the water of the bowl touched the paper? The 
tumbler, which appears empty, was actually full with air. Since air 
occupies the space inside the tumbler, water could not reach the 
paper. 
1 Activity 5.13:
 For this activity your requirements are - a deflated football, a 
balance and some sand.
 Place the deflated football on the left pan of the balance as 
shown in Fig 5.15(a). On the right pan, put sand gradually till the 
pointer is aligns the zero mark. At this stage, we may say that mass 
of the football is equal to that of the sand on the pan.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
 We have learnt that the objects around us consist of materials. A 
material is a type of matter. Different materials look different and their 
properties are also different. Some are hard while others are soft. We 
can see through some objects; but we cannot see through some other 
objects. Some are soluble in water, while some others are not. These 
properties   help in classification of objects based on their properties.
 Now inflate the football and place it on the left pan of balance. 
Does the pointer still indicate zero? Certainly not. To balance it, more 
sand has to be put on the right pan [Fig 5.15 (b)]. This means that mass 
of the inflated football is more than that of the deflated football. Hence 
we come to know that air has mass. Thus, air has mass and it occupies 
space. Therefore, air is a matter.
What you have learnt?
¿ Objects are made from different materials.
¿ Objects are classified according to their shapes, colours, hardness, 
softness etc.
¿ We can see through transparent objects or transparent materials.
¿ The objects or materials through which we cannot see is called 
opaque objects or opaque materials.
¿ Objects or materials through which we cannot see clearly are 
Classification 
Hard 
Soluble 
Insoluble
Soft
Lustrous 
Non Lustrous
Mass 
Transparent 
Opaque 
Translucent 
Volume 
Matter
Key words :

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
known as Translucent object or translucent materials.
¿ Some materials are soluble in water. On the other hand while 
some materials are insoluble in water.
¿ Some objects sink in water and some objects float.
¿ The volume of an object is the space it occupies.
¿ The amount of matter present in an object is the mass of the 
object. 
¿ Anything that has mass and volume is matter.

 (a) Gold    (b) Aluminium
 (c) Copper    (d) Bamboo

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
  Extended learning
¿  Some of the objects you use can be recycled. Gather 
information through newspapers, magazines, internet etc and 
prepare a note. Meet a person who collects scrap objects. Discuss 
the use of these objects with them.
¿ Using material that would otherwise be discarded (like 
cardboard, old newspapers, plastic etc) create a useful object (eg. 
pencil holder, small plant pot). You can decorate your objects and 
bring it to the classroom. Explain what materials are used and why 
they are suitable for your objects.
¿ Collect 20 different materials from your household and classify 
them based on properties of the material such as lustre, hardness, 
solubility, transparency etc. Remember that some objects may have 
multiple properties. Now relate the property with use of the object.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
 Let us have a look at the materials around us. Most of these are 
visible to our naked eyes ; e.g. water, soil, sand, metals, plastics, food 
items, clothes etc. Some others are invisible ; e.g, air. All these materials 
are called matter in science. Matter may be of three types – solid, liquid 
and gas. There are sugar, salt, rice, dal, tea leaves etc. in your house. 
These are solid matters (or simply solid). Water, milk, oil etc. form 
liquids. Air is of course gaseous matter.
6.1 Matter, substance and mixture
 Some of these matters consist of only one type of substance. For 
example, a piece of iron consists of iron only. Similarly, a glass of pure 
water consists of water only, and nothing else. Some others consist of 
more than one type of matter ; e.g. soil and air. Matter may be either a 
pure matter (substance) or a mixture. Now let us try to understand what 
substance and mixture are.
	 Take 	 a	 glass	 bottle	 and	 half	 fill	 it	 with	 water.	 Now	 put	 some	
sandy soil into it and stir thoroughly with a spoon. Then allow it to 
stand undisturbed for some time. You will see three distinct layers in 
the bottle as shown in the Fig. 6.1. These are the three components 
present in the soil. The top layer is formed by clay with water, the 
middle layer consists of sand while the bottom layer consists of pebbles 
(small stones). We may call each of these components a substance. Soil 
is a mixture of these substances. A substance consists of only one type 
of matter. But a mixture consists of more than one type of substances. 
Each substance in a mixture is a component of the mixture.
 Fig. 6.1: Substances present in soil (a mixture)
Clay
Sand
Pebbles
Soil (mixture)

Sand
Pebbles
Clay + Water

6.2 Why do we need separation of substances of a mixture?
Will you be able to eat chira	 (flattened	 rice)	 if	 husks	 are	 not	
removed from it ? Certainly not. What will be the consequence if small 
pebbles, generally mixed with masoor dal (lentil) or rice, are not these 
removed before cooking ? Thus, in our daily life, we see that some less 
useful materials are there with the more useful materials. Before using, 
we need to separate the components. Thus, separation of substances is 
necessary.
It is not that separation is necessary in case of food items only. 
Separation is mainly done for three purposes.
	 ¿	 To remove non-useful components :  In water treatment  
plants, insoluble impurities and microorganisms are removed 
from water to make it potable.
	 ¿ 	To obtain useful product : Sea water is evaporated to obtain 
common salt. Salt is used extensively in our kitchens and 
industries.
	 ¿	 To obtain pure material : Pure sugar crystals are obtained from 
sugarcane juice, whereas the impurities remain in the juice.
6.3 Methods of separation
Now, we will explore how we can separate substances from a 
mixture. In order to separate substances present in a mixture we need to 
know the characteristics of the components. A particular characteristic 
of	one	of	the	components	is	used.	This	may	be	difference	in	size,	shape,	
colour, weight, physical state, water soluble or not, attracted by magnet 
or not etc. The substance that needs to be separated from a mixture 
should not have the same characteristic with those present in the other 
components.

Mixture
Sand

6.3.1 Handpicking  
Have you seen your mother separating the small pebbles from 
pulses and rice? She separates by picking the pebbles with hand (Fig. 
6.2). Handpicking	is	useful	when	the	components	of	a	mixture	differ	
in	shape,	size	or	colour.	This	method	is	applicable	in	separating	small	
quantities of substances. 
Can you think of collecting the rice grains from the rice stalks by 
handpicking in large quantities?

6.3.2 Sieving 
 Sometimes a stock of rice grains may contain some amount of 
paddy seeds (dhan). Have you seen how paddy seeds are separated 
from the rice grains? Sometimes it is done by handpicking, isn’t it? 
But, if the quantity of seed is high, then what does she do? She uses a 
bamboo sieve to separate the rice grains [Fig. 6.3 (a)]. The seeds, being 
bigger	in	size	than	rice	grains,	remain	on	the	sieve.	The	rice	grains	pass	
through	the	holes	of	the	sieve.	Again,	during	preparation	of	rice	flour	
in a ‘dheki’ or grinder, another sieve is used [Fig 6.3(b) ]. The holes of 
this	sieve	are	very	small.	The	fine	particles	of	rice	grains	pass	through	
the holes of the sieve. Separation of the components of a mixture using 
sieve is termed as sieving.
 The same technique is used at construction sites to separate 
pebbles from sand. The stones present in the sand remain on the sieve 
and the sand particle passes through the holes [Fig. 6.3(c)].
Fig. 6.3 (c) : Sieving sand

Dheki

6.3.3 Threshing  
You know that farmers work hard to produce food crops for us. 
They cultivate and harvest the stalks bearing the matured seeds. Then 
they separate the seeds from the stalks. You must have seen bundles of 
paddy	stalks	lying	in	the	paddy	fields	after	harvesting.	The	stalks	are	
sun dried before the seeds are separated from them. Each stalk carries 
many seeds. Imagine the number of seeds in hundreds of bundles of 
such stalks! How does the farmer separate the seeds from the stalks?
 You may pluck mangoes or guavas from the trees. But seeds of 
various types of crops such as paddy, pulses, mustard, wheat etc are 
much smaller than mangoes or guavas. So, plucking them from their 
stalks takes lots of time. The process that is used to separate seeds 
from stalks etc. is called threshing. It is the process of beating out the 
seeds from the harvested crop plants. Perhaps you have seen threshing 
of paddy seeds from the stalks using bullocks [ ‘morona mora’, Fig. 
6.4(a)]. Threshing is also done by stomping the paddy stalks especially 
in the rainy season. Sometimes the stalks are beaten to separate the 
seeds from the stalk. Nowadays machines are used to thresh out large 
quantities of harvested seeds from the crop plants. These machines are 
called threshers.
Fig 6.4 : Threshing methods (a) by using bullocks (b) by using feet  
(c) by beating   (d) by using thresher machine

6.3.4 Winnowing 
The seeds obtained after threshing may contain some small cut 
pieces of leaves, unwanted light debrises and immature seeds. These 
unwanted components must be removed from the seeds, isn’t it? This 
is done by winnowing. This method involves separating the heavier 
components from the lighter ones with the help of wind. Just consider 
one	example.	Y ou	definitely	like	to	eat	roasted	peanuts.	Take	some	of	
them and rub between your palms. You will see the red skins being 
separated from the white peanuts. Now blow air on the mixture from 
your	mouth.	What	do	you	see?	The	red	skins,	being	lighter,	fly	away	
and the tasty peanuts remain exposed. Samething is done in winnowing. 
After threshing, the seeds are taken on a bamboo tray (‘kula’) 
for winnowing [Fig.6.5(a)]. But separating large quantities of grains 
by winnowing with bamboo tray is laborious and time consuming. 
So winnowing is done by another technique. The farmer stands on an 
elevated platform, in an open space. Then he slowly slides the mixture of 
seeds and small fragments of stalks. Seeds, being heavier, fall straight to 
the ground. The broken fragments of leaves, immature seeds, fragments 
of stalks and dust and sand particles are lighter. These fall at a little 
distance away as these are carried away by wind. Thus, pure seeds are 
obtained [Fig.6.5(b)].
Have	 you	 seen	 separating	 husks	 from	 flattened	 rice	 (chira) by 
winnowing by using a bamboo tray?
Explore : What	differences	in	the	properties	of	rice	grains,	pulses	and	
pebbles help us in separating them by the above method?

6.4 Sedimentation and Decantation 
Have you seen ever your mother washing rice grains with water 
before cooking it? She rubs the rice grains with water in a pot. Some 
impurities	 in	 the	 form	 of	 light	 particles	 float	 in	 the	 water.	 Some	 get	
mixed with water, making the water turbid. The rice grains being heavy 
settle at the bottom of the pot. Then she slowly tills the pot carefully and 
allows the water to drain away. The impurities are also drained away 
along with the water. But the rice grains remain in the pot. 
While preparing tea, generally a strainer is used to separate the 
tea leaves. If you do not have a strainer, what will you do ? Just allow 
the wet tea leaves to settle down at the bottom of the pot. Then slowly 
incline the pot holding it above another pot to transfer the supernatant 
tea. While doing this, do not jerk the pot so as to prevent the tea leaves 
from falling into the pot below. This is a very simple method, is not it ? 
Here, the tea leaves, settled at the bottom is the sediment. This process 
of separating liquid (i.e., tea) from solid (i.e. tea leaves) by removing 
the liquid part from the sediment is called decantation.
1 Activity 6.2:
Make a mixture of dry sand and powdered dry leaves. Take the 
mixture on a plate. Look at the mixture carefully. Can you distinguish 
these	two	components	easily	?	Are	the	sizes	of	particles	of	the	two	
components similar? Is it possible to separate these components by 
handpicking? Now, take your mixture on a plate to an open ground 
and stand on a raised platform. Hold the plate containing the mixture, 
at your shoulder height. Tilt it slightly, so that the mixture slides down 
slowly. What happens? Do both the components (sand and powdered 
leaves) fall at the same place? Is there a component that is blown 
away? Did the wind help to separate these two components ?

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
Fig 6.7 (a) : Sedimentation Fig 6.7 (b): Decantation
 In the above examples of rice grain and water as well as tea leaves 
and water, each one contains a solid in a liquid medium. Separation of 
these mixtures has two steps. Initially the mixture is allowed to stand 
for sometime without shaking it. The heavy substance such as rice 
grains or tea leaves settle down. The matter that settles at the bottom 
of a liquid layer is called the sediment. The process of settling down 
of heavier insoluble particles  at the bottom of a liquid layer is called 
sedimentation.
1 Activity 6.3: 
Materials required : Two beakers (or glass tumbler), one glass 
rod or spoon, water and sand.
Take a mixture of water and sand in a beaker. Stir it well using 
a glass rod. The mixture will be turbid. Now leave it undisturbed for 
some time. What do you see? Sand settles down at the bottom of the 
beaker because it is heavy. This is called sedimentation. The sand 
in this case is the sediment. [Fig. 6.7(a)]. Then pour the clear water 
layer	to	the	other	beaker	in	such	a	way	that	the	sand	does	not	flow	
down along with it. The clear water collected is called decant. By 
using this method, you have separated insoluble sand (solid) from 
water (liquid)  as shown in Fig. 6.7(b).
Water
Water
Sand

pan ? In the same way, why do we rotate the containers to remove 
the husk from the split moong dal soaked in water ?

Fig 6.8 : A mixture of oil in water
Oil
Water
6.5 Filtration 
Is decantation a perfect process of separation ? There is always a 
chance of a small amount of solid (e.g. sand) getting mixed with water. 
Similarly, during decantation of tea, some tea leaves may remain in the 
tea. Then how can we solve this problem ?
 The same principle is used for separating a mixture of two liquids 
that do not mix with each other. For example, oil and water don’t mix 
with each other. On shaking they form white turbidity. If the mixture 
of such liquids is allowed to stand for sometime, they form two layers 
(Fig. 6.8). The oil that forms the top layer can then be separated from 
the lower water layer by decantation.
Explore :	Why	does	oil	float	over	water?	Is	there	any	liquid		 	
that settles below water? Name it, if any.

You must have seen how tea is prepared. A 
strainer is used, so that the tea leaves do not fall 
down along with tea. The tea leaves remain on 
the strainer while the tea falls down. This is an 
example	of	filtration.	
In the Fig.6.1, the top layer is a clay-
water mixture. Can you separate clay from 
water by decantation ? On prolonged storage, 
the clay will get sedimented. Then you can 
decant the water to separate. But if you want 
to separate instantly, what will you do ? You 
have	to	do	filtration.

water into the funnel ?

1
	 Water 	will	pass	through	the	filter	paper	into	the	glass.	Mud	and	
other	insoluble	matters	will	remain	on	the	filter	paper.	This	is	filtration. 
The water collected in the glass is called filtrate. In broad sense, the 
filter	paper	is	called	a	filter.	
Materials required : Beaker,	glass	rod,	funnel,	filter	paper,	stand,	
a glass and muddy water.
Procedure : Take	 a	 filter	 paper.	 Fold	 it	 twice	 to	 make	 a	 cone	 	 	 	 	 	 	 	 	 	 	
[Fig. 6.10 (a)]. Fix it inside the funnel as shown in Fig.6.10(b). Wet 
the	filter	paper,	it	will	ensure	perfect	fixing	of	the	cone	inside	the	
funnel. Fix the funnel on the stand [Fig. 6.10(c)]. Keep the glass 
below the funnel. Pour muddy water into the funnel using a glass 
rod [Fig. 6.10 (c)].

Filter Paper :
Filter	paper	is	a	sieve	made	of	paper	with	fine	holes.	We	can	filter	
very small particles using this type of sieve. Can we use ordinary 
paper for this purpose? Why?
	 Y ou 	 surely	 have	 water	 filter	
in	 your	 house.	 Such	 filters	 have	 a	
candle (stick) made of ceramic. The 
candle  has very tiny pores on it. The 
unwanted particles present in the 
water can not pass through the pores. 
Only water and particles smaller 
than the pores can pass through it. 
These particles are so small that we 
can not see them by our naked eyes. 
The potable water is collected in the 
lower	part	of	the	filter	(Fig.	6.11).
 Filtration is also used in 
preparing paneer (cheese). In order to 
prepare paneer, a few drops of lemon 
juice is added to the milk when it is 
boiling. This gives a mixture of solid 
paneer and water. The paneer is then 
(a) Adding lemon juice to 
boiling milk
(b) Mixture of paneer and 
water (sedimentation)
(c) Filtration using cloth
Fig 6.12: Separation of paneer from milk
separated	by	filtering	the	mixture	through	a	fine	cloth	or	a	strainer	(Fig.	
6.12). You can do this experiment by yourself.
Fig 6.11 : Water filter

Explore: 
(i) Name the process of formation of paneer by adding lemon juice in 
milk. It is also one kind of separation of paneer from water, both of 
which are components of milk.
(ii) Consider two mixtures - one separated by sieving and the other 
separated by filtration. What is the main difference between the 
components of each mixture? Mind that both the processes require a 
separating membrane (sieve or filter paper).
1 Activity 6.5:
 You can make an arrangement to separate sand and water from 
a mixture of the two. For this, you will need a tumbler, a mixture of 
sand and water, a plastic bottle, a knife, a piece of cloth and a rubber 
band.
What will you do ?
	 Cut 	 off	 the	 upper	 part	 of	 the	 plastic	 bottle	 as	 shown	 in	 the	
following	figure.	Cover	the	cut	mouth	of	the	bottle	(upper	part)	with	
the cloth and tie it with the rubber band. This will act like a funnel. 
Place this funnel inverted on the open mouth of the lower part of the 
bottle. Then pour the mixture of sand and water on the funnel. Now 
observe and record your observation.
Rubber band
Cloth
Discussion :
Think about the following questions based on your results. 
 (i)  Why did the water in the mixture drops down to the bot -
tom of the plastic bottle? 
 (ii)  What is the name of the process of separation used?
 (iii) What kind of physical property was applied to separate the 
mixture of water and sand?
Result : Sand remains on the cloth but water gets collected at the bottom 
of the plastic bottle.

Do you know about tea bags ? 
Tea bags are used to prepare a cup of tea 
instantly. You have certainly seen tea bags. 
Small amount of tea powder is kept in a small 
packet. Initially soft cloth like silk, muslin etc 
were used to make tea bags. Silk and muslin 
are strong and do not get torn out in hot water.  
It can hold the tea powder even in hot water 
and allow water to pass through it. Nowadays 
filter	paper	is	mostly	used	in	tea	bags.
1 Activity 6.6:  
Separation of mixture of salt and water
  Why	can’t	we	filter	salt	from	salty	water?
Required materials : The two cut pieces of plastic bottle used in 
Activity 6.5, salt, water, a glass, burner, an empty can of tin and 
stand.
6.6 Evaporation
	 A 	mixture	of	water	and	sand	can	be	separated	by	filtration.	How	
can a mixture of water and salt be separated ? Can it also be separated by 
filtration?
         Tea bag
How to Do :
¿		Add a small amount of salt in water taken in a glass and stir. 
¿		Transfer the salt water to the funnel and try to seperate as 
showns in Activity 6.5. Does the salt separate? Does anything 
remain  on the cloth? Note the observations.
¿		Pour the mixture of water and salt from lower part of the   
 bottle  into the tin-can.
¿		Place the can above the burner. Heat it until the whole amount 
 of  liquid in the can evaporates.
¿		Observe the inside of the tin-can and record your    
 observations in your exercise book.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
Result :
 Firstly we have found out that when the mixture of salt and water 
was poured into the funnel, salt did not remain on the cloth. 
 Secondly, when the mixture of water and salt was heated all the 
liquid in the tin-can evaporated leaving a white substance at the bottom.
When salt-water is heated, water evaporates
Salt remains after the water is evaporated
Fig. 6.13 : Evaporation of 
   salt-water 
	 A 	mixture	of	water	and	salt	cannot	be	separated	by	filtration.	This	
is because the salt particles in water are too small and are soluble in 
water.	They	can	pass	through	the	fine	holes	of	the	filter	cloth.	Salt	in	
water can be separated by boiling salt water until the whole amount of 
water is evaporated. But salt particles don’t evaporate. Separation of 
a miscible substance from a liquid by evaporating the liquid is called 
evaporation. For example, when the mixture of water and salt in the tin-
can was heated for some time, whole amount of  the water evaporated 
as water vapour and salt was left behind.
Think about the following questions based on your result. 
1.  What was the white substance that remained in the tin-can? Why 
do you think so? 
2.  Why didn’t the salt remain on the cloth? Remember that in the last 
activity, the sand remained on the cloth.

Fig 6.14: Obtaining salt from sea water
Where does the common salt come from ?
Can you think of curry, 
fried food etc. without 
salt ? Certainly not. 
Common salt is a very 
essential commodity 
for all of us. But 
how does salt reach 
our homes ? Firstly, 
it is extracted from 
sea water. Sea water 
contains many other 
substances along with 
common salt. When sea water is allowed to stand in shallow pits, water 
slowly evaporates. After a few days, the water evaporates completely 
leaving behind the common salt mixed with some other substances 
(Fig. 6.14). Common salt is then obtained from this mixture through a 
process called purification.
6.7 Churning curd
 Milk or curd is a mixture of 
fats, proteins etc. in water. When 
milk or curd is rotated with a stirrer 
inside the pot, the fat portion comes 
up to the upper part of the pot. This 
fat is called cream. The process is 
called churning of milk or curd. 
Traditionally cream is separated 
from milk in this way (Fig. 6.15)
Explore: 
¿	Does the process of churning of milk have any similarity with 
the process of swirling of the pan for the tea leaves to settle down 
before decantation during preparation of tea ?
¿	Is there any relation between the process of churning of milk and 
rotating the split moongdal soaked in water, in a pot, for removing 
the husk ?
Fig. 6.15 : Churning of milk
N.B. Have you heard about Salt Act and Mahatma Gandhi’ s Dandi 
March? What is their linkage with collection of salt from sea water? 
Try to know about this.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
Magnetic separation 
Materials required - Sand, iron filings, magnet and a piece of paper.
Fig. 6.16 : Separation of 
magnetic materials from sand
Fig. 6.17 : Lifting of heavy magnetic materials in industries 
from other materials
Sand	+	iron	filling
Magnet
Sand
Iron 
filling
 Take a mixture of sand and iron 
filings	on	a	piece	of	paper.	Now	bring	
a magnet near to this mixture. What 
do	 you	 observe?	 Iron	 filings	 cling	 to	
the magnet and hence get separated. 
Collect	 the	 iron	 filings	 in	 a	 piece	 of	
paper. Repeat this activity till all the 
iron	 filings	 are	 separated	 from	 the	
sand. Sand will remain on the paper. 
Iron is attracted by magnet. So iron is 
called a magnetic substance. But sand 
is not attracted by magnet and so it is a 
non-magnetic substance. This method 
of separation of magnetic substances 
from non-magnetic materials using 
a magnet is known as magnetic 
separation.
 In industries, iron pieces are 
separated from other materials by this 
method (Fig.6.17).
Copyright-SCERT, Assam 
 not to be republished

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
 
 
 Matter    Substance   Mixture
  Separation methods  Hand picking, Threshing 
 Winnowing    Sieving  Sedimentation 
 Decantation    Filtration   Churning curd 
 Magnetic separation
What you have learnt ?
	¿	Matter can be a pure substance or a mixture of substances.
	¿	Substances can be separated from a mixture.
	¿	A mixture has characters of its constituents, on the basis of 
which, they are separated. 
	¿	The separation of components of a mixture is done to remove 
unwanted constituents, and to obtain useful and pure substance.
	¿	Hand picking is used to separate solid substances when their 
sizes	are	sufficiently	large.
	¿	Sieving can be used for separating larger and smaller solid 
substances in a mixture.
	¿	If mixtures have light and heavy solid substances, winnowing 
can be used for separation. 
	¿	An insoluble substance in a liquid can be separated by 
sedimentation and decantation.
	¿	Filtration is used to separate an insoluble solid substance from 
a liquid.
	¿	Evaporation is used to separate a soluble  solid substance from 
a liquid.
	¿	Magnetic substances can be separated from a non-magnetic 
substance by using a magnet.
	¿	More than one method of separation can be used to separate 
the components of mixtures with multiple components.
	¿	Some of the separation technique are traditionally practiced in 
our	 society.	 These	 are-	 Preparing	 tea;	 traditional	 water	 filter	
using sand; stone and charcoal; threshing paddy seeds using 
feet and bullocks; winnowing; use of ‘ dheki’ ‘and sieve for 
making	rice	flour,	making	sugar	and	molasses	from	sugarcane	
juice; separating husk from wet ‘moong dal’; churning of milk; 
making cottage cheese (paneer) from milk; collecting salt from 
sea water etc.
Key Words

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
Extended Learning
1. Group Activity : Separation of substances using multiple 
processes
If a mixture has only two substances as components then a sin-
gle	method	is	sufficient	to	separate	the	substances	from	the	mixture.	
However, if the mixture has more than two components, then what 
will you do? 
Consider the Fig. 6.1 given at the very beginning of this chapter. 
Can you separate the three layers of clay(with water), sand and pebbles 
present in the tumbler by handpicking?  No, because there is water 
in this mixture. Water cannot be separated by handpicking, because 
you	cannot	pick	water	by	your	fingers.	Can	you	use	winnowing?	No	
because wind cannot separate these three components. Can we think 
of sieving directly in this case? Still no, since the wet mixture cannot 
be sieved. So let us try to collect the components in pure form by 
designing a procedure. This will require involvement of the methods 
of separation discussed in this chapter. 
What do you observe in the container of soil-water mixture? 
Y ou	will	find	that	the	sand	and	pebble	settle	down	at	the	bottom	of	
the  tumbler as these are heavier. This is sedimentation. Sand par-
ticles are smaller than the pebbles and they are light. So, the clay 
portion is in the top layer mixed with water. Try to transfer the top 
layer to another container very carefully inclining the tip of the con-
tainer. See that the sediments are not disturbed and remains in the 
first	container.	This	process	is	decantation.  Thus clay (with water) 
gets separated from the sediment (sand + pebbles). 
The clay from the clay-water mixture can be separated in three 
ways. Firstly, keep the mixture undisturbed for one or two days.  The 
clay will settle at bottom of the pot. This is sedimentation. Water is 
separated it by decantation and the clay will remain in the pot. Sec-
ondly,	filter	the	clay-water	mixture	directly	using	a	cloth	to	separate	
the clay from water. This is filtration. Thirdly, boil the clay-water 
mixture until water evaporates completely leaving the clay. This is 
evaporation.
Now the only sand-pebble mixture remains. Dry the mixture 
in the Sun. It will remove any residual water. You can hand pick 
the	pebbles.	But	it	may	not	be	so	effective.	Sieve	the	mixture	using	
a household  sieve. This is sieving. This will separate sand from the 
pebbles.

Rub a magnet across the sand mass. The magnetic substances 
in the sand will stick on the magnet. Collect these on a paper. This 
is magnetic separation.	Follow	the	whole	process	shown	in	flow	
chart 6.1. This summarises all the methods applied for separation of 
the components in a soil sample systematically.

Why do sand and pebble particles settle at the bottom of the tumbler?  
Why do the pebbles settle below the sand layer ? Why does the clay 
portion get mixed with water? What will happen if the clay layer 
with water is stored for a few days?

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
When you move out of your home, your parents insist you to carry 
an umbrella the sky is cloudy. Certainly, you have the experience of 
going out without an umbrella in the morning when the sky is clear 
and coming back in the afternoon getting wet in the rain. Do you not 
like to observe the movement of clouds in the sky? You may have 
heard the elders saying that the day before was very hot during the 
day time and it became cold somewhat at night. ‘It was hot during day 
time’ is being said about the atmospheric temperature. Temperature is 
the measure of hotness or coldness of an object. Temperature is the 
reliable measurement of heat of an object. You can learn more about it 
in chapter-10 of your science textbook, part-II. You may have faced the 
sun shine, rain, lightning, thunderstorm etc. Do you know what does 
humidity mean? You must be knowing that water vapour is present in 
the air. Humidity is the amount of water vapour (moisture) present in 
the air. The temperature, amount of humidity, rainfall, wind velocity, 
direction of wind etc. keep changing, are they not? The temperature of 
the atmosphere, humidity, wind velocity etc. create a particular condition 
in the atmosphere every day. Let us discuss about this condition.
7.1 Weather
The weather is cloudy today. It may rain today. Yesterday it was 
very hot. It may be sunny tomorrow. You may have heard these types 
of discussions. Here it is being said about the weather of yesterday, 
today and tomorrow. Actually, this is the forecast of the weather. Think 
whether it will rain in all the parts of a state, district or town at a time? 
It may or may not rain in all places at the same time, is it not? The 
condition created daily in the atmosphere of a place by the atmospheric 
temperatures, humidity, amount of rainfall, wind velocity etc. is called 
the weather of that place.
 Have you listened to the weather report broadcast through television 
and radio? The weather report is also published in some newspapers. 
What is said or written in the weather report? If you listen carefully to 
the weather forecasts you will get information about the temperature, 
Weather and

Elements of weather Instruments used to measure
Air temperature Thermometer (Maximum and Minimum 
temperature)
Amount of rainfall Rain gauge
Direction of wind Wind vane
Speed of air Anemometer
Air pressure Barometer
 Data for rainfall may not be recorded everyday as there may not 
be rain always. If you get rains, you can record it. From the table you 
can easily understand that the maximum and minimum temperature, 
humidity, amount of rainfall may not be same in all the seven days 
of a week. Perhaps the maximum and minimum temperature recorded 
may or may not be the same for some days. You will surely get such 
differences.
 Temperature and humidity of the atmosphere may change every 
day. Hence the weather of a place may change every day or every 
week. This is called the change of weather. Weather is such a complex 
phenomenon that it may change within a  very short period of time. 
Have we not experienced of getting storm, rainfall, lightning suddenly? 
We are astonished when heavy rainfall vanishes within a very short 
time followed by bright sunshine. 
 Now we have understood that the temporary and local conditions 
of the atmosphere at a specific place and time, including temperature, 
humidity rainfall etc. is called the weather of the place of that time. 
Temperature, humidity and other factors are called elements of weather.
 Do you know what instruments are used by the meteorologist 
in the meteorological centre to collect data about the factors of 
weather? Usually they use the instruments given in the following 
table.
 You must have heard your elders or grandparents telling about the 
present days getting changed in climate and about higher temperature 
in present days then the days before. What is this ‘climate’ your elders 
or grandparents are talking about?

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
7.2 Climate 
 Try to recall, what you have learnt about the weather of a place 
for each day of a week by collecting the data of the factors of weather 
like temperature, humidity, rainfall etc.? We shall be able to find out the 
average of maximum and minimum temperature for seven days. It is 
also possible to find out the average weather of a  particular region for a 
long period. The average weather pattern of a place over a long period 
of time, say 25-30 years or more  is called the climate of that place. 
The mean records of weather for several decades help to determine the 
climate of a place. It means that records of the factors of weather for 
several decades help us to determine the climate of a place. Take an 
example, suppose the average temperature of a place for a period 25-30 
of years is high, then we can say that the climate of that place is hot. If 
the average rainfall is also high, then we can say that the place is hot 
and damp.
       Fig. 7.1 : Picture of desert with camel
 You have surely heard about the climate of a desert which  is hot 
and dry. It means the temperature of a desert is high for many years and 
the region also receives very less rainfall over a long period of time. 
The scientists of the meteorological department collect the information 
of weather of each day and preserve this for several decades. This 
preserved information of a place is used to determine the climate of that 
place. Means of temperature, humidity, rainfall of a place are worked 
out. Let us learn how to calculate the mean temperature of a place. First 
we find out the average of the temperature of each month of a year. 
Secondly, we calculate average of such average temperature for many 
years. This gives the mean temperature of that place. Is the atmospheric 
temperature same in all the regions of the Earth? Let us discuss.

7.3 Climatic regions of the earth
 Do you know that light of the sun does not fall equally on all 
regions of the earth? The sun does not even set at the poles of the  Earth 
for six months and does not rise for another six months. That means 
the sun only rises and falls once in a year in the poles. Polar regions are 
very cold. These regions are covered with snow.  
 Tropical region is situated in the middle of the earth. This region 
lies between Tropic of Cancer approximately in the North of the equator 
and Tropic of Capricon in the south of the equator. Most of the heat from 
the sun falls in these regions. So these regions get plenty of rainfall and 
have high temperature. North temperate climatic region is situated in 
the North of the tropical region and South temperate climatic region is 
situated in the south of the tropical region. The climate of the temperate 
region is neither hot nor cold. Seasons like summer, autumn, winter, 
spring are seen in these region.
 You must have learnt that variation in weather and climate 
occur due to the sun. You will learn about effect of climate on soil and 
organisms elaborately in higher classes. Climate influences the dress, 
food habit, habitation, livelihood, health etc. of human being. Hence 
the shape, size and structure of plants and animals living in different 
regions are found to be different.
 Climate of a region in the earth also depends upon its height from 
sea level, shape of the land and distance of the place from sea. For these 
reasons differences in climate of different regions of India are found. 
For example, the desert of Rajasthan is very hot compared to very cold 
climate of Jammu and Kashmir.
Fig. 7.2 (a) : Desert of Rajasthan

Fig. 7.2 (b) : Snow caped mountain of

7.4 Climate of Assam : Its impact on agriculture and daily life 
Assam and North-eastern region receive very heavy rains. The 
humidity of air in this region is high. Due to tropical monsoon, Assam 
experiences neither very hot nor very cold climate. Therefore, the climate 
of Assam is generally ‘tropical monsoon rainfall’ type. The people of 
this region also enjoy the change of seasons as in the temperate climatic 
region. 
 You know that the growth of all plants depends on sunlight, 
water and soil. Therefore, agriculture in any place depends on the 
weather, climate and soil of that place. The air pressure, temperature 
and rainfall vary in different seasons like summer, rainy season, autumn 
and winter. Different types of crops are grown in different seasons 
accordingly. Assam also grows different crops according to the seasons. 
During the summer and rainy season, Assam receives heavy rains due 
to southwest monsoon winds. The air temperature is also higher during 
this time. This weather is suitable for paddy, jute etc. Hence these crops 
are grown in Assam. These are called Kharif crops. Similarly, Rabi 
crops like wheat, maize and mustard are grown in the early autumn 
and winter season (dry season). Vegetables like potatoes, spinach 
etc. are usually grown in winter as they require less heat and water. 
Thus, there is a good relationship between climate and agriculture.

7.5 Effect of Climate Change :
Have you ever heard the elders talking about that present days are 
very hot compared to earlier days. From the information collected  by the 
scientists, it is known that the temperature of the atmosphere is increasing 
gradually. This increasing of temperature is the effect of climate change.
Fig. 7.3 : Dry, cracked and hard agricultural land
 Try to think whether the 
agricultural land which becomes 
dry and hard with cracks due to 
drought can yield any crops? 
Will it not lead to reduction in 
crop production? Then there 
may also be scarcity of food 
for the people. Drought may 
create storm of dust and sand. 
This storm may bring problems 
in different ways to the life of 
human being.
 You may have noticed 
that schools remain closed 
Some phenomena occuring frequently due to climate change are 
drought, storm, hot wave, raising of sea level etc. People along with 
other animals in almost all the parts of the Earth have been facing 
various problems due to the effect of climate change.You may have also 
heard that the agricultural land becoming dry, cracked and hard.
Fig. 7.3: Sand storm

Do you know ?
Emission of greenhouse gas due to excessive 
burning of fossil fuels for energy, chemical 
reactions during production in industries, use 
of electrical appliances is leading to rising 
temperatures in earth’ s atmosphere.
 Scientists have been forecasting about climate change from time 
to time. For example, increase in water level in seas, melting of glaciers 
and ice layers, global warming etc. The scientists also forecast continuous 
increase in atmospheric temperature  in the world due to increase in man 
made greenhouse gas. Drastic drought, storm, sea wave, increase in sea 
water level etc. may directly cause harm to the animals. Due to climate 
change dangerous phenomena related to weather may occur frequently. 
To reduce devastating result of such phenomena human beings should 
be cautious. The human activities related to livelihood should be suitable 
for the environment as well as climate.
when the temperature is extremely high. Heat related diseases occur 
due to extreme hot weather. It becomes difficult to go outside the home 
and move here and there for work. The increase in temperature in the 
atmosphere may lead to damage of forests caused by wild fires. It may 
also lead to destruction of the habitat of wild animals. Many regions 
suffer from shortage of water due to climate change.

SCIENCE / ¿Âõ:±ò
What you have learnt?
¿ The weather of a place is the daily condition in the 
 atmosphere caused by atmospheric temperature, humidity,  
 rainfall, wind speed etc. 
¿ Component of weather includes atmospheric temperature,  
 humidity and wind direction and  speed.
¿ The weather changes everyday or every week i.e. frequently. 
¿ The climate of a place is determined from the long term (25- 
 30 years)  average weather conditions. 
¿ All changes of weather are affected by the Sun. In the polar  
 region, effect of winter is there throughout the year. Sun does  
 not set for six months of the year and does not rise for the  
 remaining six months in the polar region. 
¿ Climate is the long-term pattern of weather conditions in any  
 region.
¿ Climate change affects every aspect of our lives.
¿ Everyone must be aware of the problems  that can be caused  
 by climate change and try to mitigate impacts of climate   
 change through life style and activities. 
Exercise
1. Answer very briefly- 
 (a) What is called weather?
 (b) What is called climate?
 (c) Name three factors of weather.
 (d) What are the two seasons experienced in a year in Assam 
with heavy rainfall ?
2. Write the difference between weather and climate.

3. Darken the circle of the correct answer.
 (i) Where are the temperate regions of the earth located? 
  (a) North pole
  (b) South pole
  (c) Equatorial 
 (d) North and South of the equatorial region, respectively. 
 (ii) Which is the kharif crop?
  (a) Paddy 
  (b) Mustard
  (c) Potatoes
 (d) Wheat

_________.

to  __________.
